\begin{split}\forall 1\leq i\leq k\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{U}(\mathsf{P}\mathsf{X}^{i})={\mathds{X}}^{\otimes e_{i}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\;,\quad\mathcal{U}(\mathsf{P}\mathsf{Z}^{i})={\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes e_{i}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,\end{split}

(91)

or equivalently in representation form

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{U}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}(\alpha)=\rho^{{\mathds{X}}}(\alpha)\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}={\mathds{X}}^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\;,\quad\mathcal{U}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}(\alpha)=\rho^{{\mathds{Z}}}(\alpha)\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}={\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(2)

As
\mathscr{S}
passes the check along the edges
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}-\mathtt{Sample}_{\cdot}
perfectly, the assignments to the
\mathsf{P}\mathsf{Z}
variables and
\mathsf{SamZ}
variables are consistent. Namely,

\forall 1\leq i\leq k\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{U}(\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot,i})=\mathcal{U}(\mathsf{P}\mathsf{Z}^{i})={\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes e_{i}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

and equivalently in projective form

\forall z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot}}_{z}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(92)

(3)

As
\mathscr{S}
passes the checks
\mathtt{Sample}_{A}-\mathtt{Intro}_{A}-\mathtt{Read}_{A}
perfectly, we can deduce that the assignments to the
\mathsf{Ans}
,
\mathsf{ReadAns}
and
\mathsf{SamAns}
variables are consistent:

\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{A}}=\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{A}}=\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Read}\mathsf{Ans}^{A}},

(93)

namely that for every
1\leq j\leq\Lambda
,
\mathcal{U}(\mathsf{SamAns}^{A,\cdot,j})=\mathcal{U}({\mathsf{Ans}^{A,\cdot,j}})=\mathcal{U}(\mathsf{ReadAns}^{A,\cdot,j})
(and similarly for
B
). In addition, as the
\mathsf{Que}^{A}
-variables are checked to be the
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
image of the
\mathsf{SamZ}^{A}
-variables, and
\mathsf{ReadQue}^{A}
are checked to be consistent with
\mathsf{Que}^{A}
, we can deduce that

\forall\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Read}\mathsf{Que}^{A}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Que}^{A}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{A}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{A}(z)=\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

where
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{A}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}
is the
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
-evaluated PVM (Definition
3.32
) associated to
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{A}}_{z}
, and the last equality is due to (
92
).

(4)

As
\mathscr{S}
passes the checks
\mathtt{Read}_{A}-\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{h}-...-\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{2}-\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{1}
perfectly, we can deduce that for every
1\leq r\leq h-1
and
\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
,

\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{r+1}\mathsf{Que}^{A}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{ReadQue}^{A}}_{[(\cdot)_{\leq r}=\mathtt{x}]}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{\leq r}(z)=\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\;,

(94)

where
(\cdot)_{\leq r}
is the
r
-prefix function (
72
) — which is part of the data of the CLM
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
— and
\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{\leq r}(z)
is as in (
70
).

(5)

As
\mathscr{S}
passes the checks
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}-\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{1}-...-\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{h}-\mathtt{Read}
perfectly, and using (
91
) and (
94
), we get for every
1\leq r\leq h
and
\mathtt{x},\nu\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
that

\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{r}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}=\mathscr{F}^{{\mathds{Z}}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{<r}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\cdot\mathscr{F}^{{\mathds{X}}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{A,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq r})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\\
\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{<r}(z)=\mathtt{x}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\\
(\mathfrak{S}_{\leq r}^{A,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(w)=\nu\end{subarray}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{w}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

where
\mathfrak{S}^{A,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j}
are again the extensions of the perpendicular maps that were defined in
Item

(3)
; also

\forall\mathtt{x},\nu\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{ReadQue}^{A}\sqcup\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{A}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{s}^{A,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(6)

Finally, according to (
93
), the
\mathsf{Ans}^{A}
-observables commute with the
\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{A}
-observables and the
\mathsf{SamZ}^{A}
-observables. Commuting with the
\mathsf{SamZ}^{A}
-observables translates to the
\mathsf{Ans}^{A}
-observables being of the form
\sum\mathscr{F}^{{\mathds{Z}}}_{z}\otimes\mathscr{A}^{z}
. Commuting with the
\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{A}
-observables implies that
\mathscr{A}^{z}
is equal to
\mathscr{A}^{z^{\prime}}
whenever
\mathfrak{s}(z)=\mathfrak{s}(z^{\prime})
. Namely (by repeating all these arguments for
B
and
\mathfrak{s}^{B}
as well), the strategy
\mathscr{S}
is honest (Definition
4.4
) when restricted to the copy of
\mathfrak{Intro}(\mathfrak{G})
in
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
. In particular, its value on
\mathfrak{Intro}(\mathfrak{G})
is the same as some quantum strategy for
\mathfrak{G}
itself.

Remark 4.23
.

A reader may notice that to achieve the above goals, we could have dropped the
\mathtt{Sample}_{\cdot}
and
\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{\cdot}
vertices altogether, and applied a more direct check (simplifying the augmentation). Though this is true, it will hinder the perfect completeness case, as we seek perfect
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategies, in particular strategies that commute along edges, which is problematic without these buffer questions.

Theorem 4.24

(Completeness and Soundness of Question Reduction)
.

Let
k
be a positive integer,
\mathscr{B}
a tuple of
n
vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
that induce an
[n,k,d]
-code (Section
3.7.2
), and
\mathfrak{G}
a tailored game satisfying
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
from the beginning of this section. Then, the question reduced game
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})=\mathfrak{QueRed}_{h}(\mathfrak{G},k,\mathscr{B})
has the following properties:

(1)

Completeness
: If
\mathfrak{G}
has a perfect
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy, then so does
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
.

(2)

Soundness
: If
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
has a strategy with value
1-\varepsilon
, then
\mathfrak{G}
has a strategy with value at least

1-O(h^{2}\cdot 2^{h}\cdot(1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\cdot\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}})\;.

(3)

Entanglement
: For every
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G}),1-\varepsilon)\geq 2^{k}\cdot\left(1-O((1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\varepsilon)\right)\cdot\mathscr{E}\big(\mathfrak{G},1-O\big(h^{2}\cdot 2^{h}\cdot(1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\cdot\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}}\big)\big)\;.

Remark 4.25
.

The underlying combinatorial game (Definition
2.25
) of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
is the same (when
\mathscr{B}
for
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
is chosen appropriately) as the question reduction applied in
[
JNV+21
]
. This means that the soundness proof therein already covers the soundness of Theorem
4.24
. Although this is true, and the reader familiar with
[
JNV+21
]
may even prefer their soundness proof, we include our own proof here. They are essentially the same, up to our proof leaning more on the observable perspective, which may be easier for readers who approach this result from the group stability community.

Although we construct a perfect
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy
\varphi
in our completeness proof, which is not something the authors of
[
JNV+21
]
were concerned about, this strategy is (essentially) the same as their perfect complete strategy (Section 8.3.2 therein). So, if one seeks more details regarding the perfect value of our strategy, then they can seek there as well.

Proof of perfect completeness
(1)

Assume that
\mathfrak{G}
has a perfect
m
-dimensional
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy
\mathscr{S}
, and let
\mathcal{U}
be its observable (and representation) form and
\mathcal{P}
be its projective form. Then, we can induce from it a perfect honest
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
for
\mathfrak{Intro}(\mathfrak{G})
, with
\mathcal{V}
being its observable form and
\mathcal{Q}
its projective form,
acting on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}
(see (
49
) and (
50
) in Definition
4.4
, and Claim
4.6
). Let us extend
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
to the other vertices so it becomes a perfect
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy for
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
. First, let
\mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}
be
\rho^{{\mathds{X}}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
and
\rho^{{\mathds{X}}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
, namely

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}(\alpha)={\mathds{X}}^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\quad,\quad\mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}(\alpha)={\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

By Claim
3.82
, it can be extended to the rest of the
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
vertices in a
\mathsf{ZPC}
-manner such that on the copy of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
in
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
it has value
1
.
The rest of the PVMs are forced on us via the consistency checks along the augmented edges (see the analysis in Remark
4.22
); recall the restriction notations from Remark
4.21
and the data processing notation from Definition
3.32
. For the sampling apparatus, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{A}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{A}}\

\displaystyle,\ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{B}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{B}}\ ,\

(95)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{A}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{B}}\ ,

(96)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Que}^{A}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{A}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}(\cdot)=\cdot]}\

\displaystyle,\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Que}^{B}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{SzmZ}^{B}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{B}(\cdot)=\cdot]}\ .

(97)

For the PVMs in the hiding apparatus, we elaborate more as the notation may be confusing. For the
\mathtt{Read}_{\cdot}
vertices, recalling the linear map
(\mathfrak{s}^{A,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}=(\mathfrak{s}^{A,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq h})^{\perp}
from
Item

(3)
and Remark
4.20
, we have

\forall\mathtt{x},\nu\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ ,\ a^{\mathfrak{R}},a^{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\Lambda}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathtt{Read}_{A}}_{\mathtt{x},a^{\mathfrak{R}},\nu,a^{\mathfrak{L}}}=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\colon\mathfrak{s}^{A}(z)=\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{x}}_{a^{\mathfrak{R}},a^{\mathfrak{L}}}\cdot\sum_{\chi\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\colon(\mathfrak{s}^{A,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\chi)=\nu}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{\chi}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

(98)

where
\mathcal{P}
is the projective form of the original perfect strategy for
\mathfrak{G}
(and similarly for
B
). Following the definition of an honest strategy, (
49
) and (
50
),
it is straightforward to check that the restrictions to the
\mathsf{ReadQue}^{\cdot}
and
\mathsf{ReadAns}^{\cdot}
variables satisfy

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{ReadQue}^{A}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Que}^{A}}\ &,\ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{ReadQue}^{B}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Que}^{B}}\ ,\ \\
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{A}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{A}}\ &,\ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{B}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{B}}\ .\end{split}

Though we claim that (
98
) is a PVM, this is not obvious from its definition — one needs to be convinced that the right sum commutes with the left sum for it to be an orthogonal projection. Let us prove that. By Corollary
4.15
,
\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{A}(z)=\mathtt{x}}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{A,\mathtt{x}}(z)=\mathtt{x}}
, and thus by Claim
4.19
the product of the two sums does commute. Moreover, as the readable variables are data processed versions of
\mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}=\rho^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
, which is diagonal, we deduce that this PVM is readably
Z
-aligned. For the
\mathsf{ReadAns}
and
\mathsf{ReadQue}
observables, as they are consistent with
\mathsf{Ans}
and
\mathsf{Que}
at
\mathtt{Intro}
, they agree with an honest strategy induced by a
\mathsf{ZPC}
one and hence consist of signed permutations. For the
\mathsf{ReadPerp}
observables, taking the inverse Fourier transform of (
98
), we have

\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{A}}(\alpha)=\sum_{\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}{\mathds{X}}^{\otimes\alpha\cdot(\mathfrak{s}^{A,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

where the sum is over all
\mathtt{x}
in the image of
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
— namely, this is a block diagonal matrix whose blocks are corners of the permutation matrices
{\mathds{X}}^{\otimes\cdot}
. Hence, if this matrix is invertible, it is a permutation matrix. As
\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}
commutes with
{\mathds{X}}^{\otimes\alpha\cdot(\mathfrak{s}^{A,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}}
(by Corollary
4.15
and Claim
4.19
), it is its own inverse, which proves this is indeed a signed permutation PVM.

We are left to define the PVMs at the
\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j}
vertices for
1\leq j\leq h
. This is done in a similar way to the
\mathtt{Read}_{\cdot}
vertices, and is forced on us by the consistency checks along the augmented edges. In projective form,

\begin{split}\forall\mathtt{x},\nu\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{j}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}&=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\cdot\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{A,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\\
\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{<j}(z)=\mathtt{x}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\\
(\mathfrak{S}^{A,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}(\alpha)=\nu\end{subarray}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{\alpha}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .\end{split}

(99)

By Corollary
4.15
,
\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{<j}(z)=\mathtt{x}
if and only if
\mathfrak{S}^{A,\mathtt{x}}_{<j}(x)=\mathtt{x}
. Hence, by Claim
4.19
, this is indeed a PVM. Actually, Claim
4.19
shows that this strategy commutes along all
\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j}-\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j+1}
edges, as
\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{\bf\leq j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{S}^{A,\mathtt{x}}_{\bf\leq j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}
commutes with
\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{A,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}
according to it. Furthermore, in a similar manner to the
\mathtt{Read}
-vertex PVM, we can deduce that this is a readably
Z
-aligned signed permutation PVM.
All in all,
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{Q}\}
is indeed a
\mathsf{ZPC}
-strategy (all non-hide to non-hide edges are clearly commuting). The fact that this strategy is perfect can be checked by the reader, with the help of the analysis of perfect strategies in Remark
4.22
.
This finishes the perfect completeness proof.

Proof of soundness
(2)
and entanglement lower bound
(3)

The idea in the soundness proof is to perturb an almost perfect strategy for
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
to become a strategy that passes all edges of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
perfectly, except for the single edge of
\mathfrak{Intro}(\mathfrak{G})
.
The way we perturb
\mathscr{S}
to be perfect on all edges (except for
\mathtt{Intro}_{A}-\mathtt{Intro}_{B}
), roughly follows the analysis of such strategies in Remark
4.22
.

Claim 4.26
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\{\mathcal{U}\}
be an
N
-dimensional strategy for
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
with value
1-\varepsilon
. Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=(1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\varepsilon
. Then, there is a strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{W}\}
, acting on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}
, such that:

1.

(Almost Perfect)
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
has value of at least
1-O(\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\prime}})
;

2.

(Perfect on Pauli basis)
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
passes the edges from the copy of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
perfectly;

3.

(Uses a specific representation of the Pauli group)
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
satisfies

\mathcal{W}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}=\rho^{\mathds{X}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{W}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}=\rho^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

where
\rho
is the representation specified in Definition
3.68
.

Proof.

As the copy of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
is played with probability
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
when running
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
, the restriction of
\mathscr{S}
to the vertices of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
passes it with probability of at least
1-4\varepsilon
. Hence, by Claim
3.83
, Fact
3.73
and Claim
3.22
, there is a partial isometry
\omega\colon\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}
such that

\begin{split}\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ \left\|\omega\mathcal{U}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}(\alpha)\omega^{*}-{\mathds{X}}^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\right\|_{hs}^{2}&\;,\quad\left\|\omega\mathcal{U}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}(\alpha)\omega^{*}-{\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\right\|_{hs}^{2}\leq O(\nicefrac{{k^{2}\varepsilon}}{{d^{2}}})\\
&\textrm{and}\\
1-\tau(\omega^{*}\omega)&\;,\quad\ 1-\tau(\omega\omega^{*})\leq O(\nicefrac{{k^{2}\varepsilon}}{{d^{2}}})\;\end{split}

(100)

As
\varepsilon^{\prime}=(1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\varepsilon
, the above quantities are all
O(\varepsilon^{\prime})
. Letting
\mathcal{W}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}(\alpha)={\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}(\alpha)={\mathds{X}}^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
, we can use Claim
3.82
to extend
\mathcal{W}
to a perfect strategy for
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
. As
1-\tau(\omega^{*}\omega),1-\tau(\omega\omega^{*})\leq O(\varepsilon^{\prime})
, we can use orthogonalization (Fact
3.21
) and extend
\mathcal{W}
such that for every non-
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
vertex
\mathtt{x}
,

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\colon S_{\mathtt{x}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}\ \colon\ \ \|\omega\mathcal{U}^{\mathtt{x}}(\alpha)\omega^{*}-\mathcal{W}^{\mathtt{x}}(\alpha)\|_{hs}^{2}\leq O(\varepsilon^{\prime}).

(101)

Now
\mathcal{W}
induces a representation on all vertices of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
, and is thus a strategy for it. We already assured that
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{W}\}
passes the copy of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
perfectly, and we chose it such that
\mathcal{W}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{{\mathds{X}}}}=\rho^{\mathds{X}}\otimes{\rm Id}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{{\mathds{Z}}}}=\rho^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}
. Hence, conditions
2.
and
3.
are satisfied. Finally, equations (
100
) and (
101
) imply that across any non-
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
edge, the observables of
\mathcal{W}
are
O(\varepsilon^{\prime})
-close to those of
\mathcal{U}
, hence they pass all these edges with probability at most
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\prime}})
worse than
\mathcal{U}
(Claim
3.29
).
This implies
\mathrm{val}(\mathscr{S}^{\prime},\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G}))\geq 1-O(\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\prime}})
, finishing the proof.
∎

Claim 4.27
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be a strategy for
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
with value
1-\varepsilon
, where
\mathcal{U}
is its observable form and
\mathcal{P}
its projective from, that acts on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Moreover, assume it passes the copy of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
with probability
1
, and satisfies
\mathcal{U}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}=\rho^{\mathds{X}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}=\rho^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
, or equivalently in projective form
\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
. Then, there is a strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
, with
\mathcal{Q}
being its projective form and
\mathcal{W}
its observable form, such that:

1.

(Almost Perfect)
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
has value
1-O(\sqrt{h^{3}\cdot\varepsilon})
;

2.

(Agrees on Pauli basis)
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
agrees with
\mathscr{S}
on the copy of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
;

3.

(Readable variables are consistent with
{\mathds{Z}}
-measurements)
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall z,\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ ,\ r\in[h]\ \colon\quad\ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{A}}_{z}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{B}}_{z}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Read}\mathsf{Que}^{A}}_{\mathtt{x}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Que}^{A}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{A}(z)=\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Read}\mathsf{Que}^{B}}_{\mathtt{y}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Que}^{B}}_{\mathtt{y}}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{B}(\cdot)=\mathtt{y}]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{B}(z)=\mathtt{y}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{r}\mathsf{Que}^{A}}_{\mathtt{x}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{<r(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}}]}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{<r}(z)=\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{r}\mathsf{Que}^{B}}_{\mathtt{y}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{B}_{<r(\cdot)=\mathtt{y}}]}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{B}_{<r}(z)=\mathtt{y}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

Namely, all consistency checks on the readable variables in
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
are satisfied.

Proof.

As
\mathscr{S}
has value
1-\varepsilon
, and each edge in the sequence
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}-\mathtt{Sample}_{\cdot}-\mathtt{Intro}_{\cdot}-\mathtt{Read}_{\cdot}-\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{h}-...-\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{1}
is sampled with probability
\Omega(\frac{1}{h})
, we can deduce that
\mathscr{S}
passes each such edge with probability of at least
1-O(h\varepsilon)
. Specifically, from the consistency check along
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}-\mathtt{Sample}_{\cdot}
together with the fact that small inconsistency implies closeness of PVMs (Proposition
3.12
), we can deduce that

\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot}}\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(102)

By the comparison along
\mathtt{Sample}_{\cdot}-\mathtt{Intro}_{\cdot}
together with Observation
3.34
, we can deduce that

\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(\cdot)=\cdot]}\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}\ .

(103)

In combination with (
102
) and the semi-triangle inequality for PVMs (Item
2.
in Proposition
3.12
), we get

\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(\cdot)=\cdot]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(104)

Just as a sanity check for the reader, the closeness claim on PVMs in (
104
) means, for the
A
vertices, that

\sum_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\Big\|\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Que}^{A}}_{\mathtt{x}}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\\
\mathfrak{s}^{A}(z)=\mathtt{x}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\Big\|_{hs}^{2}\leq O(h\varepsilon)\ .

By the comparison along
\mathtt{Intro}_{\cdot}-\mathtt{Read}_{\cdot}
, the
\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}
and
\mathsf{ReadQue}^{\cdot}
observables are highly consistent with each other, and as high consistency implies closeness, we have

\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{ReadQue}^{\cdot}}\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}\ .

(105)

In combination with (
104
) and the semi-triangle inequality, we get

\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Read}\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(\cdot)=\cdot]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(106)

By the consistency checks along
\mathtt{Read}_{\cdot}-\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{h}-...-\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{2}-\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{1}
combined with (
106
), and using
h
-many times the semi-triangle inequality, we get that

\forall 1\leq r\leq h\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{r}\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}\approx_{O(h^{3}\cdot\varepsilon)}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}_{<r}(\cdot)=\cdot]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(107)

We can now describe the perturbed strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
, with projective form
\mathcal{Q}
and observable form
\mathcal{W}
. First, it agrees with
\mathscr{S}
on
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
vertices (and thus satisfies condition
2.
).
Then, let

\displaystyle\forall z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\

\displaystyle\colon\ \ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot}}_{z}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

(108)

\displaystyle\forall\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\

\displaystyle\colon\ \ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Read}\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(z)=\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

(109)

\displaystyle\forall 1\leq r\leq h,\ \forall\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\

\displaystyle\colon\ \ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{r}\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}_{<r}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}_{<r}(z)=\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(110)

Note that if we extend this choice of
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
to a quantum strategy, then condition
3.
is satisfied, which means the only condition left to be verified (after
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
is fully defined) is that it has value at least
1-O(\sqrt{h^{3}\cdot\varepsilon})
. Let us complete the definition of
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
: For the rest of the
\mathcal{W}
-observables in the vertices where changes were made, we are going to use Claim
4.9
to change the
\mathcal{U}
-observables so they commute with our choices in (
108
), (
109
) and (
110
). Let us demonstrate this analysis for two vertices,
\mathtt{Sample}_{\cdot}
and
\mathtt{Read}_{\cdot}
, as for the rest it is essentially the same type of argument. For
\mathtt{Sample}_{\cdot}
, as closeness in
L^{1}
for representations implies closeness in
L^{\infty}
(Claim
3.22
), we have

\begin{split}\forall\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\Lambda},\ \beta\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\quad{\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes\beta}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})&\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot}}(\beta)\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\\
&=\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot}}(\beta)\\
&\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\cdot{\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes\beta}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\;,\end{split}

where the approximations are due to (
102
) and Claim
3.22
, while the middle equality is due to the fact
\mathcal{U}
is a quantum strategy (and thus the observables at the same vertex commute).
Hence, by Claim
4.9
(or by Orthonormaliztion
3.21
), there are observables

\forall\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\Lambda}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\ ,

which induce a representation of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2\Lambda}
that commutes with
{\mathds{Z}}^{\beta}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
for every
\beta\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
, and

\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}}).

For
\mathtt{Read}_{\cdot}
, we have that for every
\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\Lambda}
and
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
,

\begin{split}\rho^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}]}(\gamma)\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{\cdot}\sqcup\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}},\beta)&\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadQue}^{\cdot}}(\gamma)\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{\cdot}\sqcup\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}},\beta)\\
&=\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{\cdot}\sqcup\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}},\beta)\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadQue}^{\cdot}}(\gamma)\\
&\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{\cdot}\sqcup\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}},\beta)\cdot\rho^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}]}(\gamma)\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .\end{split}

Thus, we can apply Claim
4.9
again to obtain a representation
\mathcal{W}
on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2\Lambda+k}
that commutes with the PVM
\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}(\cdot)=\cdot]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
and satisfies

\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{\cdot}\sqcup\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}},\beta)\approx_{O(h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{\cdot}\sqcup\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}},\beta)

for every
\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}}
and
\beta
. The change in the
\mathtt{Hide}
vertices depends on the closeness parameter achieved in (
107
), which is
O(h^{3}\cdot\varepsilon)
. All in all, the constructed strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
is
O(h^{3}\cdot\varepsilon)
-close to the original one
\mathscr{S}
, and thus by Claim
3.29
it has value of at least
1-O(\sqrt{h^{3}\cdot\varepsilon})
against
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
, proving clause
1.
and completing the proof.
∎

Claim 4.28
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\{\mathcal{P}\}
be a strategy for
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
, acting on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}
, satisfying:

1.

(Almost Perfect)
\mathrm{val}(\mathscr{S};\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G}))\geq 1-\varepsilon
;

2.

(Perfect on Pauli basis and all readable variables)
\mathscr{S}
passes all
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
edges perfectly, and in addition satisfies

\begin{split}\forall z,\chi\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ &\colon\ \ \mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}}_{z}=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot}}_{z}=\mathscr{F}_{z}^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\quad,\quad\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}_{\chi}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{\chi}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,\\
\forall\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ &\colon\ \ \mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Read}\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}=\sum_{v\colon\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(v)=\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{v}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,\\
\forall 1\leq r\leq h,\ \forall\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ &\colon\ \ \mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{r}\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x}}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}_{<r}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}=\sum_{z\colon\mathfrak{s}_{<r}^{\cdot}(z)=\mathtt{x}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .\end{split}

(111)

Then, there is another strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{Q}\}
for
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
acting on the same space
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}
, satisfying:

1.

(Almost Perfect)
\mathrm{val}(\mathscr{S}^{\prime},\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G}))\geq 1-O(h\cdot 2^{h}\cdot\sqrt{\varepsilon})
;

2.

(Agrees with
\mathscr{S}
on
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
vertices as well as
\mathsf{SamZ}
and
\mathsf{Que}
-variables) For all
\mathtt{x}\in{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
,
\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{x}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathtt{x}}
, and in addition the PVM
\mathcal{Q}^{\cdot}_{\cdot}
satisfy the same conditions as
\mathcal{P}^{\cdot}_{\cdot}
in (
111
).

3.

(Passes all non-
\mathtt{Intro}_{A}-\mathtt{Intro}_{B}
edges perfectly)

\begin{split}\forall a^{\mathfrak{R}},a^{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\Lambda}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}_{a^{\mathfrak{R}},a^{\mathfrak{L}}}&=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Sam}\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}_{a^{\mathfrak{R}},a^{\mathfrak{L}}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{Read}\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}_{a^{\mathfrak{R}},a^{\mathfrak{L}}},\\
\forall\mathtt{x},\nu\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{ReadQue}^{\cdot}\sqcup\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}&=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\cdot\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z,\chi\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\colon\\
\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(z)=\mathtt{x},\ (\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\chi)=\nu\end{subarray}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{\chi}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m},\\
\forall r,\ \forall\mathtt{x},\nu\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\mathcal{Q}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{r}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}&=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<r}^{\cdot}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\cdot\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}_{\leq r}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z,\chi\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\colon\\
\mathfrak{s}_{<r}^{\cdot}(z)=\mathtt{x},\ (\mathfrak{S}_{\leq r}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\chi)=\nu\end{subarray}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{z}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{\chi}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,\end{split}

(112)

where
(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}
are the extended perpendicular maps to the seeded CLMs as defined in
Item

(3)
and Remark
4.20
.

Proof.

Recall that every augmented edge in
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
is sampled with probability
\Omega(\frac{1}{h})
. For ease of following the proof, let us denote by
C>0
the universal constant induced by
\Omega(\frac{1}{h})
, namely the probability of sampling any augmented edge is at least
\frac{1}{Ch}
and hence
\mathscr{S}
passes every such edge with probability of at least
1-Ch\varepsilon
. The reader can check that for the probability distribution on edges we fixed for
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
, each augmented edge is sampled with probability of at least
\frac{1}{2h+4}
, so
C=6
is enough. The reason we use an abstract constant instead of
6
, is that we later change the distribution over edges in
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
(see Example
4.39
and specifically Figure
14
) so that every augmented edge is sampled with probability of at least
\frac{1}{4h+63}
, in which case
C=67
is enough. In any case, let us treat
C
as an unknown constant.

First, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{1}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}&=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{1}\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x}}\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{1}\mathsf{Perp}^{\cdot}}_{\nu}\\
&=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[(\cdot)_{<1}=\mathtt{x}]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\cdot\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{1}\mathsf{Perp}^{\cdot}}_{\nu}\\
&\simeq_{Ch\varepsilon}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[(\cdot)_{<1}=\mathtt{x}]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\cdot\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}_{\leq 1}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\\
&=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[(\cdot)_{<1}=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq 1})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,\end{split}

where the first equation is by definition for a projective measurement, the second and last equations use the assumptions from (
111
) on the PVMs at
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}
and
\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{1}
, and the inconsistency in the middle is due to
\mathscr{S}
passing
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}-\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{1}
with probability
1-Ch\varepsilon
. Hence, by the translation of consistency to closeness (Proposition
3.12
), we deduce that

\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{1}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}\approx_{2Ch\varepsilon}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[(\cdot)_{<1}=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq 1})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(113)

We now establish the following inductive step. Assume that for some
1\leq j<r
it holds that

\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j-1}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}\approx_{\delta}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j-1}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j-1})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ ,

(114)

for some
\delta\geq 2Ch\varepsilon
. Then it follows that

\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}\approx_{4\delta}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(115)

To show the implication
\eqref{eq:abcdefghijk}\implies\eqref{eq:abcdefghijk22222}
, we first use the fact that
\mathscr{S}
passes
\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j-1}-\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j}
with probability of at least
1-Ch\varepsilon
to write

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{x},\mathtt{x}^{\prime},\nu,\nu^{\prime}\\
(\mathtt{x})_{<j-1}=\mathtt{x}^{\prime}\\
\nu=(\mathfrak{S}_{j}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\nu^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\tau\Big(\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j-1}}_{\mathtt{x}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}\Big)\geq 1-Ch\varepsilon\ .

(116)

Using the projectivity of
\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{r}}
(for every
r\in[h]
) and our assumptions in (
111
),

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{r}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{r}\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x}}\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{r}\mathsf{Perp}^{\cdot}}_{\nu}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}_{<r}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{r}\mathsf{Perp}^{\cdot}}_{\nu}\ .\end{split}

For every
\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
, let

A^{\mathtt{x}}_{\nu}=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{j-1}\mathsf{Perp}^{\cdot}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{j})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\colon(\mathfrak{S}_{j}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\nu^{\prime})=\nu}\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{j-1}\mathsf{Perp}^{\cdot}}_{\nu}\ .

This is a data processed version of
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{j-1}\mathsf{Perp}^{\cdot}}
, but the exact function through which we are evaluating depends on the seed
\mathtt{x}
. Then, (
116
) can be rewritten as

\sum_{\mathtt{x},\nu}\tau\left(\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Hide}^{j}\mathsf{Perp}^{\cdot}}_{\nu}A^{\mathtt{x}}_{\nu}\right)\geq 1-Ch\varepsilon\ ,

which means that
\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}
is
Ch\varepsilon
-inconsistent with
\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}A^{\mathtt{x}}_{\nu}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}
— note that this is a POVM but not necessarily a projective one. In general, if one has three PVMs,
B
and
C
with outcomes in
X
, and
D
with outcomes in
Y
, then
B\approx_{\delta}C
implies
DBD\approx_{\delta}DCD
. Hence, using (
114
), we can deduce that

\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}A^{\mathtt{x}}_{\nu}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\approx_{\delta}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{j})^{\perp}\circ(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j-1})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}=\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\ ,

where the equation uses both that, as defined in Remark
4.20
,
(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{j})^{\perp}\circ(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j-1})^{\perp}=(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}
, as well as the fact that
\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}
commute.
Combined with the above, we have

\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}\approx_{2Ch\varepsilon}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}A^{\mathtt{x}}_{\nu}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\approx_{\delta}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\ ,

which implies (
115
) using the semi-triangle inequality for closeness of POVMs and the fact
\delta\geq 2Ch\varepsilon
.

This establishes the desired implication
\eqref{eq:abcdefghijk}\implies\eqref{eq:abcdefghijk22222}
. Together with the base case (
113
), we deduce that

\forall j\in[h]\ \colon\ \ \mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{j}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}\approx_{4^{j}Ch\varepsilon}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}_{<j}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{S}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{\leq j})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(117)

By the consistency check along
\mathtt{Hide}_{\cdot}^{h}-\mathtt{Read}_{\cdot}
, we deduce from (
117
) that

\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{ReadQue}^{\cdot}\sqcup\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}_{\mathtt{x},\nu}\approx_{4^{h+1}Ch\varepsilon}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{X}}_{[(\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}(\cdot)=\nu]}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

(118)

Let us choose the following signed permutation representations of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
,

B^{\cdot}(\alpha)=\sum_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\mathscr{F}^{\mathds{Z}}_{[\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot}(\cdot)=\mathtt{x}]}{\mathds{X}}^{\otimes\alpha\cdot(\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}}\ ,

where, as usual,
\alpha\cdot(\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}
is the left multiplication of the row vector
\alpha
with the matrix
(\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}
— this is indeed a matrix as
(\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}
is a linear map for every fixed
\mathtt{x}
. By applying the inverse Fourier transform to (
118
), one can deduce that

\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}\approx_{4^{h+1}Ch\varepsilon}B^{\cdot}\ .

(119)

By the consistency checks along
\mathtt{Sample}_{\cdot}-\mathtt{Intro}_{\cdot}-\mathtt{Read}_{\cdot}
, we deduce that

\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Sam}\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}\approx_{Ch\varepsilon}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}\approx_{Ch\varepsilon}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Read}\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}\ .

(120)

Hence, using the “small
L^{1}
-distance between representations implies small
L^{\infty}
distance” proved in Claim
3.22
twice, combined with (
111
) and (
120
), we have

\displaystyle\forall\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\Lambda}\ ,\ \beta\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ {\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes\beta}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})

\displaystyle\approx_{6Ch\varepsilon}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot}}(\beta)\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{SamZ}^{\cdot}}(\beta)

\displaystyle\approx_{6Ch\varepsilon}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\cdot{\mathds{Z}}^{\otimes\beta}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}\ .

Similarly, using (
119
), (
120
) and Claim
3.22
, we have

\displaystyle\forall\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\Lambda}\ ,\ \beta\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\ \colon\ \ B^{\cdot}(\beta)\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})

\displaystyle\approx_{6\cdot 4^{h+1}Ch\varepsilon}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\beta)\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})

\displaystyle\approx_{6Ch\varepsilon}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\beta)\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadAns}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\beta)

\displaystyle\approx_{6Ch\varepsilon}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{ReadPerp}^{\cdot}}(\beta)

\displaystyle\approx_{6\cdot 4^{h+1}Ch\varepsilon}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{R}},\alpha^{\mathfrak{L}})\cdot B^{\cdot}(\beta)\ .

Note that, as
B^{\cdot}
and
\rho^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
are both signed permutation representations, the group generated by their images is finite. We can thus apply Claim
4.9
where
G
is the group generated by the images of
B^{\cdot}
and
\rho^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
(which also fixes the representation of
G
in the claim), and
A
is
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2\Lambda}
with representation
\psi=\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot}}
; this gives us two representations
\theta^{A},\theta^{B}
of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2\Lambda}
such that
\theta^{A}\approx_{O(4^{h}h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{A}}
,
\theta^{B}\approx_{O(4^{h}h\varepsilon)}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{B}}
and
\theta^{\cdot}
perfectly commutes with
B^{\cdot}
and
\rho^{\mathds{Z}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{m}
.

Combining all of the above, if we let
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{V}\}=\{\mathcal{Q}\}
satisfy (
111
), (
112
) and

\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{A}}=\theta^{A}\quad,\quad\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{Ans}^{B}}=\theta^{B}\ ,

then
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
satisfies clause
2.
and
3.
from the requirements of this claim, as well as being
O(4^{h}\cdot h\cdot\varepsilon)
-close to
\mathscr{S}
. By applying Claim
3.29
, we can deduce clause
1.
as well.
∎

To conclude the soundness proof, we need to combine the three preceding claims:

1.

Given a strategy
\mathscr{S}
acting on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
that has value
1-\varepsilon
, we can apply Claim
4.26
on it to get a strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
that acts on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}
, passes the
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
-vertices perfectly and has value of
1-O(\sqrt{(1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\varepsilon})
. Moreover, the strategies are
O((1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\varepsilon)
-close on
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}
and
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}
observables, which means in particular that
1-\frac{N}{2^{k}\cdot m}\leq O((1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\varepsilon)
and thus
N\geq(1-O((1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\varepsilon))2^{k}\cdot m
.

2.

The strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
satisfies the assumptions of Claim
4.27
, and thus there is a strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}
for
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
that behaves well on all
\mathsf{Que}
-variables and has value
1-O\left(h^{3/2}\cdot((1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\varepsilon)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\right)
.

3.

The strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the assumptions of Claim
4.28
, and thus there is a strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}
which passes all edges of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
perfectly (except for maybe
\mathtt{Intro}_{A}-\mathtt{Intro}_{B}
), and has value of at least
1-O(h^{2}\cdot 2^{h}\cdot(1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\cdot\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}})
, which proves the soundness in
Item

(2)
.
In addition, the resulting strategy is honest (Definition
4.4
). Hence, by Claim
4.6
, such a strategy induces a strategy for
\mathfrak{G}
with the same value which acts on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Hence,
m\geq\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{G},1-O(h^{2}\cdot 2^{h}\cdot(1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\cdot\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}}))
, and we can conclude that

\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G}),1-\varepsilon)\geq 2^{k}\cdot\left(1-O((1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\varepsilon)\right)\cdot\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{G},1-O(h^{2}\cdot 2^{h}\cdot(1+\nicefrac{{k^{2}}}{{d^{2}}})\cdot\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}}))\ ,

which proves the entanglement lower bound
Item

(3)
.

4.5
Applying question reduction to a tailored normal form verifier

Up until now, we discussed a certain combinatorial transformation that takes as input an integer
k
, a set
\mathscr{B}=\{w_{1},...,w_{N}\}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
, and a game
\mathfrak{G}
(with certain assumptions on its sampling mechanism and answer length functions), and outputs a new game
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
(defined in Section
4.4
), which is a specific augmented sum of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}(\mathscr{B})
from Section
3.8.3
and
\mathfrak{Intro}(\mathfrak{G})
from Section
4.1
.

For the proof of Compression (Theorem
2.53
), one needs a way of applying this combinatorial transformation on the level of tailored normal form verifiers (TNFVs), as was described in Theorem
4.1
. Namely, we seek a transformation on a pair consisting of an integer
\lambda
and a TNFV
\mathcal{V}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
that outputs a new TNFV
\mathsf{QuestionReduction}(\mathcal{V},\lambda)=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}_{\textsc{QR}},\mathcal{A}^{\lambda}_{\textsc{QR}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},\mathcal{D})
, such that on the combinatorial level the
n^{\rm th}
game of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
is the question reduced
{2^{n}}
-
{\rm th}
game of
\mathcal{V}
. So,

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}=\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}},k(n,\lambda),\mathscr{B}(n,\lambda))

for some integer-valued function
k(n,\lambda)
and function
\mathscr{B}(n,\lambda)
valued in tuples of vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k(n,\lambda)}
.

Recall that for
\mathfrak{QueRed}_{h}(\mathfrak{G},k,\mathscr{B})
to have the desired properties of Theorem
4.24
, several non-trivial assumptions about the inputs need to be satisfied:

(1)

The game
\mathfrak{G}
is tailored, with an underlying
h
-level CL sampling scheme (Definition
4.16
); namely, its underlying graph’s vertex set is
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
and its distribution over edges
\mu
is the pushforward of the uniform distribution over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
through a fixed pair of
h
-level conditionally linear maps (Definition
4.13
)
\mathfrak{s}=(\mathfrak{s}^{A},\mathfrak{s}^{B})\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
.

Note that in the definition of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
we always insisted that this parameter
r
, controlling the number of vertices in
\mathfrak{G}
, to be equal to
k
, which is the length of answers at the
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}
and
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}
vertices in the generalized Pauli basis game
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}(\mathscr{B})
. But, and it is straightforward to check, we only needed
k
to be larger or equal to
r
— that is because we can pre-compose
(\mathfrak{s}^{A},\mathfrak{s}^{B})
with the restriction to the first
r
coordinates
{\rm rest}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
and proceed accordingly. All in all, we need the sampling procedure of
\mathfrak{G}
to be induced by two CLMs
\mathfrak{s}^{A},\mathfrak{s}^{B}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
and for
r\leq k
.

As the input game
\mathfrak{G}
is assumed to have a sampling procedure induced by an
h
-level CLM, we need the TNFV
\mathcal{V}
that we manipulate to be such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the
n^{\rm th}
game
\mathcal{V}_{n}
has an underlying
h
-level sampling scheme. In addition, every transformation that we apply on
\mathcal{V}
, e.g.
\mathsf{QuestionReduction}
, should retain this property. Hence, we are going to move to a subcategory of TNFVs that have this property.

(2)

In addition to the sampling procedure, we needed
\mathfrak{G}
to have constant length functions
\ell^{\mathfrak{R}},\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\to\mathbb{N}
. Namely, there is some integer
\Lambda
such that for all
\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
the functions satisfy
\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathtt{x})=\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}(\mathtt{x})=\Lambda
. This turns out to be an easy condition to satisfy, and even if the normal form verifier does not satisfy it, a
padding
transformation can be applied on it so that it does (see Section
4.5.4
).

(3)

We aim to “question reduce”, which translates to making the underlying graph of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
exponentially smaller than that of
\mathfrak{G}
, i.e., the number of questions in it needs to be
\poly(r)
.
57
57

57

Actually, it is enough to be quasi-polynomial in
r
, which is the parameter setup used in
[
JNV+21
]
.
As
\mathfrak{Intro}(\mathfrak{G})
contributes
2
vertices, and the augmentation adds on
2h+4
more
58
58

58

For the proof of Compression,
h
can be upper bounded by
5
(see Remark
4.35
). So this can be thought of as a constant number of vertices.
, most of the vertices in the underlying graph of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
come from
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
. In
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
, there are
2+2N+18N^{2}
vertices, where
N=|\mathscr{B}|
.
In addition, for the soundness and entanglement lower bounds proved before (
Item

(2)
and
Item

(3)
of Theorem
4.24
) to have any meaning, we need the parameter
\nicefrac{{k}}{{d}}
to be bounded, where
d
is the distance of the code induced by the set
\mathscr{B}
.
A tradeoff arises: For the distance of the code defined by
\mathscr{B}
to be large enough, the set itself needs to be large enough (in particular larger than
k
). But, on the other hand, the larger
\mathscr{B}
is the larger the underlying graph of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
is. Finally, there should be an efficient way of calculating, given
i\in[N]
, the vector
w_{i}\in\mathscr{B}
. This point is solved completely by the existence of good error correcting codes with an efficient algorithm to calculate their encoding matrix (Fact
3.72
).

(4)

As part of the application of
\mathfrak{QueRed}
, we used quite a lot of structure regarding the CLMs
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
and
\mathfrak{s}^{B}
. We assumed to have access to the spaces
W^{\mathtt{x}}_{r}
, the
j^{\rm th}
step
\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{j}
of the CLMs calculation for every
1\leq j\leq h
and seed
\mathtt{x}
, and also to the
perpendicular
functions
(\mathfrak{s}^{\cdot,\mathtt{x}}_{j})^{\perp}
. To handle this intricacy, we are going to change the definition of a sampler (Definition
2.39
) so that it can describe further its “inner working”.

4.5.1
The category of
h
-level tailored normal form verifiers

Definition 4.29

(
h
-level conditionally linear sampler)
.

Let
h
be a positive integer. An
h
-level conditionally linear sampler (CL sampler) is a
6
-input deterministic Turing machine
\mathcal{S}
that satisfies the following restrictions.
First, the input to
\mathcal{S}
is expected to be

(n,{\rm Action},{\rm Player},j,\mathtt{x},z)\ ,

where
n
and
j
are positive integers in binary,
{\rm Action}
is taken from the set

\{{\rm Dimension,\ Register,\ Marginal,\ Evaluate,\ Perpendicular}\}\ ,

{\rm Player}
is taken from the set
\{A,B\}
, and
\mathtt{x},z
are bit strings (interpreted as vectors in some finite vector space).
Second, for every positive integer
n
, there must exist an integer
r=r(n)
and two
h
-level conditionally linear maps (CLMs, Definition
4.13
)
\mathfrak{s}^{A}(n),\mathfrak{s}^{B}(n)=\mathfrak{s}^{A},\mathfrak{s}^{B}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, such that
\mathcal{S}

encodes
this pair of functions:

1.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(n,{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, then it outputs (the binary encoding of)
r(n)
.

2.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(n,{\rm Register},{\rm Player},j,\mathtt{x},\cdot)
, then it outputs the
j^{\rm th}
register subspace with respect to the seed
\mathtt{x}
, namely
W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}

(
71
) with respect to
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Player}
. As
W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}
is a register subspace, the way it is encoded is by providing the indicator of the set
I^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}\subseteq[r]
, which says what are the standard basis vectors that span
W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}
. Note that the indicator of
I^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}
is just a bit string of length
r
.

3.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(n,{\rm Marginal},{\rm Player},j,\cdot,z)
, then it outputs the
j^{\rm th}
prefix of
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Player}
’s evaluation of
z
, namely
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Player}_{\leq j}(z)
.

4.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(n,{\rm Evaluate},{\rm Player},j,\mathtt{x},z)
, then it outputs the
j^{\rm th}
-register output of
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Player}
evaluated on
z
given the seed
\mathtt{x}
, namely
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Player,\mathtt{x}}_{j}(z^{W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}})
. Recall that
\mathfrak{s}^{{\rm Player},\mathtt{x}}_{j}\colon W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}\to W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}
is the linear function which controls the
j^{\rm th}
step in the calculation of
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Player}
, given that the calculation up to this point produced
\mathtt{x}_{<j}
.

5.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(n,{\rm Perpendicular},{\rm Player},j,\mathtt{x},z)
, then it outputs the
j^{\rm th}
-register output of
(\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Player,\mathtt{x}})^{\perp}
evaluated on
z
given the seed
\mathtt{x}
, namely
(\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Player,\mathtt{x}}_{j})^{\perp}(z^{W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}})
. Recall that the maps
(\mathfrak{s}^{{\rm Player},\mathtt{x}}_{j})^{\perp}\colon W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}\to W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}
are some fixed linear function whose rows are spanning the subspace perpendicular to the rows of
\mathfrak{s}^{{\rm Player},\mathtt{x}}_{j}
.
59
59

59

Note that these maps were not part of the definition of a CLM, but were assumed to be part of the data needed for question reduction in the beginning of Section
4.4
. As Remark
4.30
notes, there is a canonical way of extracting such maps from the rest of the CLMs data.

Remark 4.30
.

A few things to note about the differences between the above definition and
[
JNV+21
, Definition 4.14]
: They use the Action name “Linear” instead of “Evaluate”. Furthermore, they do not include the Perpendicular action — This is because there is a canonical (and efficient) way of calculating linear maps
(\mathfrak{s}^{{\rm Player},\mathtt{x}}_{j})^{\perp}
from the rest of the possible outputs of the sampler, as described in
[
JNV+21
, Section 8.2]
clause
6.
in page
94
, which is the detailed description of the decider of their introspective verifier.

Remark 4.31

(Dimension of CL sampler bounded by running time)
.

Since, given inputs such as
(n,{\rm Marginal,\ Player},j,\cdot,z)
, the CL sampler
\mathcal{S}
needs to output an
r(n)
-long bit string, where
r(n)=\mathcal{S}(n,{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, it is immediate that
r(n)\leq\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
.

Remark 4.32
.

Our original sampler from Definition
2.39
is a randomized TM, while the
h
-level sampler is a deterministic one. To extract the output of the original sampler out of an
h
-level sampler, we can — though it is not part of the definition of a CL sampler — attach a
{\rm Sample}
action to the list. Given
(n,{\rm Sample},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
,
\mathcal{S}
runs as follows:

1.

First, it calls
\mathcal{S}(n,{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
to obtain
r
.

2.

Then, it samples
r
random bits to obtain
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
(which makes
\mathcal{S}
back to a randomized TM, but only with respect to this
{\rm Sample}
action).

3.

It then calls
\mathcal{S}(n,{\rm Marginal},A,h,\cdot,z)
to obtain
\mathtt{x}=\mathfrak{s}^{A}_{\leq h}(z)=\mathfrak{s}^{A}(z)
, and
\mathcal{S}(n,{\rm Marginal},B,h,\cdot,z)
to obtain
\mathtt{y}=\mathfrak{s}^{B}_{\leq h}(z)=\mathfrak{s}^{B}(z)
.

4.

Finally, it outputs (the encoding of)
\mathtt{x}\sqcup\mathtt{y}
.

Note that, indeed, this is what we expect a TM to do to be able to sample from the distribution induced by the pair
(\mathfrak{s}^{A},\mathfrak{s}^{B})
.

Definition 4.33

(Tailored
h
-level normal form verifier)
.

An
h
-level tailored normal form verifier
(
h
-level TNFV) is a quadruple of Turing machines
\mathcal{V}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
,
where
\mathcal{S}
is an
h
-level conditionally linear sampler as in Definition
4.29
,
\mathcal{A}
is an answer length calculator as in Definition
2.41
,
\mathcal{L}
is a linear constraint processor as in Definition
2.43
, and
\mathcal{D}
is the canonical decider as in Definition
2.45
.

Such a TNFV is
\lambda
-bounded
, for a positive integer
\lambda
, if

•

The running times (Definition
2.36
) of
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{L}
are all bounded by
n^{\lambda}
, namely

\forall{n}\in\{0,1\}^{*}\ \colon\ \ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\ ,\ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};{n},\cdot,\cdot)\ ,\ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{L};{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\ \leq\ n^{\lambda}.

•

The description length (Definition
2.37
) of
\mathcal{V}
is bounded by
\lambda
, namely
|\mathcal{V}|\leq\lambda
.

Similar to Definition
2.48
, when
\mathcal{V}
is a
\lambda
-bounded
h
-level TNFV, then there is an associated
n^{\rm th}
game to it for every
n\geq 2
. Similar to Remark
2.49
, the
n^{\rm th}
game of an
h
-level TNFV may be well defined even if it is not
\lambda
-bounded. Actually, all we need is for

•

\mathcal{A}(n,\mathtt{x},\kappa)
to halt whenever
\mathtt{x}
is of length
r(n)=\mathcal{S}(n,{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
and
\kappa\in\{\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{L}\}
;

•

\mathcal{L}(n,\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y},a^{\mathfrak{R}},b^{\mathfrak{R}})
needs to halt whenever
\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y}
are of length
r(n)
, and
a^{\mathfrak{R}}
and
b^{\mathfrak{R}}
are of length
|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(n,\mathtt{x},\mathfrak{R}))|
and

|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(n,\mathtt{y},\mathfrak{R}))|
respectively.

Note that by assuming
\mathcal{S}
is an
h
-level CL sampler (Definition
4.29
), we are guaranteed that it behaves well, in particular it always halts (on relevant inputs), and there are associated CLMs underlying it. So, no additional assumptions on
\mathcal{S}
are needed.

We now have all the definitions required to formulate the version of compression (Theorem
2.53
) which is proved in this paper. Recall the asymptotic notation from Remark
1.2
.

Theorem 4.34

(Compression of
h
-level tailored normal form verifiers)
.

For every positive integer
h
, there exist two positive integers

c=c(h)\quad{\rm and}\quad C=C(h)

that depend only on
h
, and a
2
-input Turing machine
\mathsf{Compress}_{h}
, that takes as input a
h
-level
TNFV
\mathcal{V}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
and a positive integer
\lambda
(in binary), and outputs a
5
-level
TNFV
\mathsf{Compress}_{h}(\mathcal{V},\lambda)=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda},\mathcal{A}^{\lambda},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},\mathcal{D})
, such that:

•

Sampler properties
: The
5
-level CL sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}
depends only on
\lambda
and
h
, (but not the specific
\mathcal{V}
), and
\mathsf{Compress}_{h}
can calculate its description in time
\polylog_{h}(\lambda)
;
60
60

60

Recall our asymptotic notation from Remark
1.2
to parse
\polylog_{h}
.
in particular,
|S^{\lambda}|\leq c\log^{c}\lambda
. In addition,
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}
runs in
\poly_{h}(n,\lambda)
-time, namely

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\ \colon\ \ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda};n)\leq c\cdot(n^{c}+\lambda^{c})\ .

•

Answer length calculator properties
:
\mathcal{A}^{\lambda}
depends only on
\lambda
and
h
, and
\mathsf{Compress}_{h}
can calculate its description in time
\polylog_{h}(\lambda)
; in particular
|\mathcal{A}^{\lambda}|\leq c\log^{c}\lambda
. In addition,
\mathcal{A}^{\lambda}
runs in
\poly_{h}(n,\lambda)
-time, namely

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\ \colon\ \ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A}^{\lambda};n,\cdot,\cdot)\leq c\cdot(n^{c}+\lambda^{c})\ .

Finally, given that
\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
, where
r(n)=\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}(n,{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, and that
\kappa\in\{\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{L}\}
, the output of
\mathcal{A}^{\lambda}(n,\mathtt{x},\kappa)
never decodes (Definition
2.34
) to an
\mathfrak{error}
sign.

•

Linear constraints process properties
:
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
depends on both
\lambda
and
\mathcal{V}
, and
\mathsf{Compress}_{h}
can calculate its description in time
\poly_{h}(\log\lambda,|\mathcal{V}|)
; in particular,
|\mathcal{L}^{\prime}|\leq c\cdot(\log^{c}\lambda+|\mathcal{V}|^{c})
. In addition,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
runs in
\poly_{h}(n,\lambda)
-time, namely

\forall{n}\in\mathbb{N}\ \colon\ \ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime};{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\ \leq\ c\cdot(n^{c}+\lambda^{c})\ .

•

Decider properties
: The canonical decider
\mathcal{D}
(Definition
2.45
) is fixed and runs in time which is linear in its input length.

•

Value properties
: If
\mathcal{V}
is
\lambda
-bounded, then

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, the output of
\mathsf{Compress}_{h}
, satisfies: For all
n\geq C
,

1.

Completeness
: If
\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}}

has a perfect
Z
-aligned permutation strategy that commutes along edges (
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy), then so does
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}
.

2.

Soundness
:

\mathscr{E}(\mathcal{V}_{n}^{\prime},\frac{1}{2})\geq\max\left\{\mathscr{E}(\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}},\frac{1}{2}),2^{2^{\lambda n}-1}\right\}
.

Remark 4.35
.

The above version of compression, Theorem
4.34
, is the one proven in this paper. By choosing
h=5
, and using
\mathsf{Compress}_{5}
from the above theorem instead of
\mathsf{Compress}
from Theorem
2.53
,
\mathsf{TailoredMIP}^{*}=\mathsf{RE}
(Theorem
2.31
) can still be deduced exactly as in Section
2.6
.

In a similar way, we now describe the version of Theorem
4.1
which is proved in this section. Recall again the asymptotic notation from Remark
1.2
.

Theorem 4.36

(
h
-level Question Reduction)
.

Let
h
be a positive integer. There exists a positive integer

c=c_{\textsc{QR}}(h)

(121)

that depends only on
h
, and a
2
-input Turing machine
\mathsf{QuestionReduction}_{h}
that takes as input an
h
-level TNFV
\mathcal{V}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
and a positive integer
\lambda
(in binary), and outputs a new
3
-level
TNFV

\mathsf{QuestionReduction}_{h}(\mathcal{V},\lambda)=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}_{\textsc{QR}},\mathcal{A}^{\lambda}_{\textsc{QR}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},\mathcal{D})

such that:

•

Sampler properties
:
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}_{\textsc{QR}}
depends only on
\lambda
and
h
(and not the specific
\mathcal{V}
), and
\mathsf{QuestionReduction}_{h}
can calculate its description in time
\polylog_{h}(\lambda)
; in particular,
|S_{\textsc{QR}}^{\lambda}|\leq c\log^{c}\lambda
. In addition,
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}
runs in
\poly_{h}(n,\lambda)
-time, namely

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\ \colon\ \ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S}_{\textsc{QR}}^{\lambda};n)\leq c\cdot(n^{c}+\lambda^{c})\ .

•

Answer length calculator properties
:
\mathcal{A}_{\textsc{QR}}^{\lambda}
depends only on
\lambda
and
h
, and
\mathsf{QuestionReduction}_{h}
can calculate its description in time
\polylog_{h}(\lambda)
; in particular
|\mathcal{A}_{\textsc{QR}}^{\lambda}|\leq c\log^{c}\lambda
. In addition,
\mathcal{A}^{\lambda}
runs in
\exp_{h}(n,\lambda)
-time, namely

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\ \colon\ \ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A}^{\lambda};n,\cdot,\cdot)\leq 2^{c\cdot(n^{c}+\lambda^{c})}\ .

Finally, given that
\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
, where
r(n)=\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}_{\textsc{QR}}(n,{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, and that
\kappa\in\{\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{L}\}
, the output of
\mathcal{A}^{\lambda}_{\textsc{QR}}(n,\mathtt{x},\kappa)
never decodes (Definition
2.34
) to an
\mathfrak{error}
sign.

•

Linear constraints process properties
:
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
depends on
\lambda,h
and
\mathcal{V}
, and
\mathsf{QuestionReduction}_{h}
can calculate its description in time
\poly_{h}(\log\lambda,|\mathcal{V}|)
; in particular,
|\mathcal{L}^{\prime}|\leq c\cdot(\log^{c}\lambda+|\mathcal{V}|^{c})
. In addition,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
runs in
\exp_{h}(n,\lambda)
-time, namely

\forall{n}\in\mathbb{N}\ \colon\ \ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime};{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\ \leq\ 2^{c\cdot(n^{c}+\lambda^{c})}\ .

Note that the running time upper bound itself is independent of the specific
\mathcal{V}
.

•

Value properties
: If
\mathcal{V}
is
\lambda
-bounded, then

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, the output of
\mathsf{QuestionReduction}_{h}
, satisfies: For all
n\geq 2
,

1.

Completeness
: If
\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}}
has a perfect
Z
-aligned permutation strategy, then so does
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}
.

2.

Soundness
: For every
\varepsilon>0
, if
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}
has a value
1-\varepsilon
strategy, then
\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}}
has a value
1-c\cdot\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{16}}}
strategy.

3.

Entanglement
: For every
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathscr{E}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n},1-\varepsilon)\geq(1-c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot 2^{2^{\lambda n}}\cdot\mathscr{E}(\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}},1-c\cdot\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{16}}})\ .

Remark 4.37
.

As claimed in the theorem, the output of
\mathsf{QuestionReduction}_{h}
is always a
3
-level
normal form verifier, regardless of what
h
was. The original level
h
plays a role in the underlying graph of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, as well as in the exact soundness guarantees, governed by
c_{\textsc{QR}}(h)
(
121
).

4.5.2
Typed conditionally linear sampling schemes

Though we focus on the category of games induced by (two)
h
-level CLMs — and respectively on
h
-level TNFVs — it will often be easier to describe the underlying sampling scheme of our games in a slightly different manner.

Definition 4.38
.

A game
\mathfrak{G}
is said to have a
h
-level typed conditionally linear sampling scheme
if its underlying graph
(V,E)
and measure
\mu
on edges are defined as follows: There is a positive integer
r
and a set
\mathcal{T}
— which we call the
type set
, and its elements are called
types
— such that
V=\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
. In addition, there is a subset
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
which induces a graph structure
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
, and an
h
-level CLM
\mathfrak{s}^{t}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
associated to every type
t\in\mathcal{T}
. Then, a pair in
V=\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
is sampled by taking a uniformly random seed
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
and a uniformly random edge
tt^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
and outputting the pair
(t,\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z)),(t^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{t^{\prime}}(z))
— namely, the pair of types are chosen uniformly from
\mathcal{E}
, and the seed defines the right coordinates by evaluating the CLM of the chosen types on it.

Example 4.39
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a game with
h
-level CLMs acting on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
controlling its sampling scheme, and let
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
be a set of size
2^{m}
for some
m\geq\log k
.
Then, the sampling scheme of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})=\mathfrak{QueRed}_{h}(\mathfrak{G},k,\mathscr{B})
which was described in Section
4.4
, is (up to some constant factor that depends only on
h
) a
1
-level typed conditionally linear sampling scheme. To see that, the type set
\mathcal{T}
consists of

\begin{split}\forall 1\leq j\leq h\ \colon\ \ &\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{j}\ ,\ \mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{j}\ ,\\
&\mathtt{Intro}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Intro}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{B}\ ,\\
&\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}\ ,\ \mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}\ ,\ \mathtt{X}\ ,\ \mathtt{Z}\ ,\ \mathtt{First}\ ,\ \mathtt{Second}\ ,\ \mathtt{Both}\ ,\\
\forall 1\leq a\leq 3\ ,\ 1\leq b\leq 3\ \colon\ \ &\mathtt{var}_{ab}\ ,\ \mathtt{row}_{a}\ ,\ \mathtt{col}_{b}\ .\end{split}

All in all,
2h+28
types. They are connected according to Figure
14
— though not drawn in the figure, we assume
all loops
appear in the type graph, namely for any type
t
of the above
2h+28
types, in addition to the edges in the figure, the edge
tt
is also present.

\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}
\mathtt{X}
\mathtt{First}
\mathtt{Both}
\mathtt{Second}
\mathtt{Z}
\mathtt{var}_{11}
\mathtt{var}_{12}
\mathtt{var}_{13}
\mathtt{var}_{21}
\mathtt{var}_{22}
\mathtt{var}_{23}
\mathtt{var}_{31}
\mathtt{var}_{32}
\mathtt{var}_{33}
\mathtt{row}_{1}
\mathtt{col}_{1}
\mathtt{row}_{2}
\mathtt{col}_{2}
\mathtt{row}_{3}
\mathtt{col}_{3}
\mathtt{Intro}_{A}
\mathtt{Intro}_{B}
\mathtt{Sample}_{A}
\mathtt{Sample}_{B}
\mathtt{Read}_{A}
\mathtt{Read}_{B}
\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{1}
\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{h}
\ddots
\mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{1}
\mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{h}
\ddots

Figure 14:
The type graph of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
. Though not drawn, all self loops are also edges in this type graph. So, in total, there are
2h+28
vertices and
4h+63
edges in this type graph.

Now, the
1
-level CLMs (i.e., linear maps) associated to the types act on the space
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2m}
, which has (not surprisingly) the same cardinality as
\mathscr{B}\times\mathscr{B}
. For all non Pauli basis types, i.e.,

\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{j}\ ,\ \mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{j}\ ,\ \ \mathtt{Intro}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Intro}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{B}\ ,

and for
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}
and
\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}
, the associated CLM is the
0
-function. Namely, for every such type
t
,

\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ \mathfrak{s}^{t}(u,v)=(\vec{0},\vec{0})\ .

For all other types
t
except for
\mathtt{X},\mathtt{Z}
, i.e., types from the list

\mathtt{row}_{a}\ ,\ \mathtt{col}_{b}\ ,\ \mathtt{var}_{ab}\ ,\ \mathtt{First}\ ,\ \mathtt{Second}\ ,\ \mathtt{Both}\ ,

the associated CLM is the identity map. Namely,

\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ \mathfrak{s}^{t}(u,v)=(u,v).

Finally, for
\mathtt{X}
we have

\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ \mathfrak{s}^{\mathtt{X}}(u,v)=(u,\vec{0}).

and for
\mathtt{Z}
we have

\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ \mathfrak{s}^{\mathtt{Z}}(u,v)=(\vec{0},v).

In this perspective, the vertices of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
are from
\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}
. But the vertices with a positive probability to be sampled belong to the following strict subset:

\begin{split}\forall 1\leq j\leq h\ \colon\ \ &(\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{j},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{j},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\\
&(\mathtt{Intro}_{A},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Intro}_{B},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Read}_{A},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Read}_{B},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\\
&(\mathtt{Sample}_{A},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Sample}_{B},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ \\
\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ &(\mathtt{X},u,\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Z},\vec{0},v)\ ,\\
\forall 1\leq a,b\leq 3\ ,\ u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ &(\mathtt{var}_{ab},u,v)\ ,\ (\mathtt{row}_{a},u,v)\ ,\ (\mathtt{col}_{b},u,v)\ ,\\
\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ &(\mathtt{First},u,v)\ ,\ (\mathtt{Second},u,v)\ ,\ (\mathtt{Both},u,v)\ .\end{split}

It is straightforward to compare these vertices to the ones we originally had when defining
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
:

1.

The vertices

\begin{split}\forall 1\leq j\leq h\ \colon\ \ &(\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{j},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{j},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Intro}_{A},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Intro}_{B},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Read}_{A},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ \\
&(\mathtt{Read}_{B},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Sample}_{A},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Sample}_{B},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\end{split}

are the same vertices as

\begin{split}\forall 1\leq j\leq h\ \colon\ \ &\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{j}\ ,\ \mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{j}\ ,\ \mathtt{Intro}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Intro}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{A}\ ,\\
&\mathtt{Read}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}\ ,\ \mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}\ ,\end{split}

in the original description.

2.

The vertices

\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ (\mathtt{X},u,\vec{0})\ ,\ (\mathtt{Z},\vec{0},v)\ ,

are the same as the vertices

\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ \mathtt{X}^{u},\ \mathtt{Z}^{v}\ ,

in the original description of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
in Section
3.8.3
. This makes sense as the set
\mathscr{B}
is of size
N=2^{m}
which can be parametrized by
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}
.
61
61

61

When we defined
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
we used
i,j
to parametrize the elements of
\mathscr{B}
instead of
u,v
.

3.

The vertices

\forall 1\leq a,b\leq 3\ ,\ u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ (\mathtt{var}_{ab},u,v)\ ,\ (\mathtt{row}_{a},u,v)\ ,\ (\mathtt{col}_{b},u,v)\ ,

are the same as the vertices

\forall 1\leq a,b\leq 3\ ,\ u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ \mathtt{var}_{ab}^{u,v}\ ,\ \mathtt{row}_{a}^{u,v}\ ,\ \mathtt{col}_{b}^{u,v}\ ,

from the
(u,v)
-th copy of the anti-commutation game (or its nullified version)
\mathfrak{M}^{u,v}
used in the definition of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
.

4.

The vertices

\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ (\mathtt{First},u,v)\ ,\ (\mathtt{Second},u,v)\ ,\ (\mathtt{Both},u,v)\ ,

are the same as the vertices

\forall u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}\ \colon\ \ \mathtt{First}^{u,v}\ ,\ \mathtt{Second}^{u,v}\ ,\ \mathtt{Both}^{u,v}\ ,

from the
(u,v)
-th copy of the commutation game (or its nullified version)
\mathfrak{C}^{u,v}
used in the definition of
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{k}
.

The distribution on edges of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{G})
induced by this typed
1
-level CL sampling scheme is not
exactly
the one we used before, but the probabilities are the same up to some constant factor (which depends on
h
). For example, the probability of sampling
\mathtt{Intro}_{A}-\mathtt{Intro}_{B}
in this setup is
\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}=\frac{1}{63+4h}
, which is lower than the
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
we had before, while the probability of sampling
\mathtt{var}^{u,v}_{11}-\mathtt{row}_{1}^{u,v}
is
\frac{1}{(63+4h)\cdot 2^{2m}}
, which may be lower or higher than the
\frac{1}{72\cdot 2^{2m}}
it was before (depending on
h
).
Though these distributions differ, the new distribution samples an edge with probability of at least
\frac{1}{63+4h}
times the old probability. Thus, all of our soundness arguments (in which the distribution played a role) are the same up to a constant depending on
h
.

4.5.3
Detyping

As Compression (Theorem
4.34
) begins and ends with an
h
-level normal form verifier, and not with typed ones, we ought to have a method of
detyping
a sampling scheme in a way that preserves most of the properties of the original game. To that end we make the following definition.

Definition 4.40

(Combinatorial Detyping)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a game with a
h
-level typed CL sampling scheme (Definition
4.38
), where the type graph is
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
, and where the CLMs
\mathfrak{s}^{t}
act on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
. The
detyped version
of
\mathfrak{G}
, which we denote by
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
, is a game with an
(h+2)
-level non-typed CL sampling scheme (Definition
4.16
), with CLMs
\mathfrak{s}^{A},\mathfrak{s}^{B}
acting on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4|\mathcal{T}|+r}
, and which are defined as follows:
First, the type
t\in\mathcal{T}
is encoded as a string

\textrm{enc}(t)=({\bf 1}_{t},\sum_{t^{\prime}\sim t}{\bf 1}_{t^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}\ ,

namely the first vector is the indicator of
t
, and the second vector is the indicator of neighbors of
t
in the graph
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
. Given

(u_{type},u_{neighbors},v_{type},v_{neighbors},z)\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\ ,

\mathfrak{s}^{A}
operates as follows:

1.

First,
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
applies the identity on
u_{type},u_{neighbors}
.

2.
