Then, there are two options — either there exists a
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that
(u_{type},u_{neighbors})=\textrm{enc}(t)
, or not. If not, then
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
zeros out the rest of the registers, namely outputs
(u_{type},u_{neighbors},\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{0})
. If there is such a
t\in\mathcal{T}
,
then it zeroes out all
v_{type}
and all coordinates of
v_{neighbors}
except the
t^{\rm th}
one.

3.

Lastly, if the partial computation of the first two steps resulted in
(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t})\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}
, then it applies
\mathfrak{s}^{t}
on the seed
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, and otherwise zeroes
z
out.

The CLM
\mathfrak{s}^{B}
acts similarly with the roles of
u
and
v
swapped. Namely:

1.

First,
\mathfrak{s}^{B}
applies the identity on
v_{type},v_{neighbors}
.

2.

Then, there are two options — either there exists a
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
such that
(v_{type},v_{neighbors})=\textrm{enc}(t^{\prime})
, or not. If not, then
\mathfrak{s}^{B}
zeros out the rest of the registers, namely outputs
(\vec{0},\vec{0},v_{type},v_{neighbors},\vec{0})
. If there is such a
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
,
then it zeroes out all
u_{type}
and all coordinates of
u_{neighbors}
except the
t^{\rm th}
one.

3.

Lastly, if the partial computation of the first two steps resulted in
(\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t^{\prime}},\textrm{enc}(t^{\prime}))\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}
, then it applies
\mathfrak{s}^{t^{\prime}}
on the seed
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, and otherwise zeroes
z
out.

Given
t\in\mathcal{T}
, the vector
(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t},\mathtt{x})
is called
the
A
-copy of
(t,\mathtt{x})
, and similarly
(\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t^{\prime}},\textrm{enc}(t^{\prime}),\mathtt{y})
is called
the
B
-copy of
(t^{\prime},\mathtt{y})
.
A vector of the form
(u_{type},u_{neighbors},\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{0})
is called an
A
-player anchor vertex, and a vector of the form
(\vec{0},\vec{0},v_{type},v_{neighbors},\vec{0})
is called a
B
-player anchor vertex.
62
62

62

As will soon be described, whenever an anchor vertex is sampled, the game always accepts.
In the case of detyping, these vertices are added just for the CL sampling structure to be attained. Later in this paper, the idea of anchoring a game is used for parallel repetition — see Section
6
.

Note that the above sampling procedure produces a pair with at least one anchor vertex, unless there is an edge
tt^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
in the type graph such that
(u_{type},u_{neighbors},v_{type},v_{neighbors})=(\textrm{enc}(t),\textrm{enc}(t^{\prime}))
, in which case

\mathfrak{s}^{A}(\textrm{enc}(t),\textrm{enc}(t^{\prime}),z)=(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t},\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z))\quad,\quad\mathfrak{s}^{B}(\textrm{enc}(t),\textrm{enc}(t^{\prime}),z)=(\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t^{\prime}},\textrm{enc}(t^{\prime}),\mathfrak{s}^{t^{\prime}}(z))\ ,

namely this is an edge between the
A
-copy of
(t,\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z))
and the
B
-copy of
(t^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{t^{\prime}}(z))
. All in all, the only vertices that have a positive probability of being sampled are those
\cdot
-player “anchor” and “copy” vertices.

For the length functions, if
\mathtt{w}
is a vertex of
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
which is the
A
or
B
copy of a vertex
(t,\mathtt{x})
in
\mathfrak{G}
, then
\ell_{\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})}^{\cdot}(\mathtt{w})=\ell_{\mathfrak{G}}^{\cdot}(t,\mathtt{x})
. Namely, the length of the copies of any vertex in the detyped game is the same as in the original game. For the rest of the vertices, the length functions are zero.

For the decision procedure: If one of the vertices of the sampled edge is an anchor vertex, then the game accepts no matter what the answers are. Otherwise, the seed was of the form
(\textrm{enc}(t),\textrm{enc}(t^{\prime}),z)
, and thus the sampled edge is between the
A
-copy of
(t,\mathfrak{s}^{z}(t))
and the
B
-copy of
(t^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{t^{\prime}}(z))
; in this case,
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
checks the answers at these vertices as if they were from
\mathfrak{G}
, and decides accordingly. In addition, if
t=t^{\prime}
, then the detyped game will also check consistency, namely that the answers at the
A
-copy of
(t,\mathfrak{s}^{z}(t))
and the
B
-copy of the same vertex
(t,\mathfrak{s}^{z}(t))
are the same.

Remark 4.41

(The double cover embeds in the detyped game)
.

Note that the game
\mathfrak{G}
does not embed in
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
— every vertex
(t,\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z))\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
is mapped to two vertices,

(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t},\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z))\quad\textrm{and}\quad(\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t},\textrm{enc}(t),\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z))\ .

But, by restricting the detyped game
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
only to such vertices (i.e.,
A
-copies and
B
-copies of vertices from
\mathfrak{G}
), we get a copy of the
double cover

\mathfrak{DoubleCover}(\mathfrak{G})
(Definition
3.52
) of the game
\mathfrak{G}
. Moreover, this copy is played with probability of at least
2^{-4|\mathcal{T}|}
(and otherwise, one of the sampled vertices is an anchor one, which means it automatically accepts).

The above remark leads us to the following immediate corollary.

Corollary 4.42

(Completeness and soundness of the detyped game. Cf. Lemma 6.18 in
[
JNV+21
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a tailored game with a
h
-level conditionally linear sampling scheme, type graph
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
, and CLMs which act on a space of dimension
r
. Then, letting
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
be the detyping of
\mathfrak{G}
as in Definition
4.40
, we have the following:

•

\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
is a tailored game with an
(h+2)
-level conditionally linear sampling scheme.

•

(Completeness) If
\mathfrak{G}
has a perfect
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy, then so does its detyping
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
.

•

(Soundness) If the detyped game
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
has a value
1-\varepsilon
strategy, then the double cover
\mathfrak{DoubleCover}(\mathfrak{G})
(Definition
3.52
) of the original game
\mathfrak{G}
has a value
1-O(2^{4|\mathcal{T}|}\varepsilon)
strategy.

•

(Entanglement) In addition,

\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{G}^{\prime},1-\varepsilon)\geq\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{DoubleCover}(\mathfrak{G}),1-O(2^{4|\mathcal{T}|}{\varepsilon}))\ .

Proof sketch.

The claim item is clear and follows by inspection. In particular, the description of
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
and
\mathfrak{s}^{B}
in Definition
4.40
make them clearly
(h+2)
-level.
The soundness and entanglement lower bound are immediate from Remark
4.41
. For completeness, recall that the double cover has a perfect
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy given that
\mathfrak{G}
has one (Claim
3.54
). Every such strategy extends, in a
\mathsf{ZPC}
manner, to the rest of the vertices of
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
— as the lengths at all the other vertices is
0
. As the decision in
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
always accepts when one of the endpoints of the sampled edges is an anchor, the resulting
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy for
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
is indeed perfect.
∎

Corollary 4.43
.

If in the type graph
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
underlying the sampling scheme of
\mathfrak{G}
, all self loops
tt
are edges in
\mathcal{E}
, then the detyped game
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
satisfies the following strengthened soundness and entanglement lower bound conditions: If
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
has a value
1-\varepsilon
strategy, then the original game
\mathfrak{G}
(and not its double cover) has a value
1-O(|\mathcal{T}|\cdot 2^{2|\mathcal{T}|}\cdot\sqrt{\varepsilon})
strategy of the same dimension, which implies

\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{G}^{\prime},1-\varepsilon)\geq\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{G},1-O(|\mathcal{T}|\cdot 2^{2|\mathcal{T}|}\cdot{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}))\ .

Proof.

Recall that if
\mathfrak{G}
has a typed
h
-level sampling scheme (Definition
4.38
), then it samples edges as follows: It chooses a uniform edge of types
tt^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
, and a uniform
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, and returns the edge
(t,\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z))(t^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{t^{\prime}}(z))
. This means that the marginal distribution
\mu(t,\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z))
is

\Probability_{z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}[\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z^{\prime})=\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z)]\cdot\frac{|\{t^{\prime}\neq t\mid tt^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}\}|+|\{t^{\prime}\neq t\mid t^{\prime}t\in\mathcal{E}\}|+1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\;,

while the probability
\mu((t,\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z))(t,\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z)))
of choosing this loop is

\Probability_{z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}[\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z^{\prime})=\mathfrak{s}^{t}(z)]\cdot\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\ .

Hence, for every
\mathtt{x}\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
(with positive probability of being sampled) we have

\frac{\mu(\mathtt{x}\mathtt{x})}{\mu(\mathtt{x})}\geq\frac{1}{2|\mathcal{T}|+1}\ .

Combining Corollary
4.42
and Claim
3.54
finishes the proof.
∎

As for all of our combinatorial transformations, we need to implement them on the level of normal form verifiers to prove compression. Thus, we need to define the
typed
version of an
h
-level CL sampler, which will underlie some normal form verifier instead of the
usual

h
-level sampler (Definition
4.29
). The definitions are very similar. The main difference is that the following encodes a sequence of CLMs parametrized by the vertices of a fixed finite type graph
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
, instead of just two sequences of CLMs. Actually, the following can be seen as a generalization of the non-typed
h
-level sampler, where in that case the type graph consists of two vertices
A
and
B
with a single oriented edge
AB
between them.

Definition 4.44

(Typed
h
-level conditionally linear sampler)
.

Let
h
be a positive integer. Colloquially, a
h
-level typed conditionally linear sampler
(typed CL sampler)
\mathcal{S}
with underlying type graph
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
is, essentially, an
h
-level conditionally linear sampler (Definition
4.29
), but instead of the
{\rm Player}
action having only
2
possible inputs, it has
|\mathcal{T}|
inputs.

Formally,
\mathcal{S}
is a
6
-input deterministic Turing machine that satisfies some additional properties. First, its input is expected to be

(n,{\rm Action},{\rm Type},j,\mathtt{x},z)\ ,

where
n
and
j
are positive integers in binary,
{\rm Action}
is taken from the set

\{{\rm Graph},\ {\rm Dimension,\ Register,\ Marginal,\ Evaluate,\ Perpendicular}\}\ ,

{\rm Type}
is taken from the set
\mathcal{T}
, and
\mathtt{x},z
are bit strings (interpreted as vectors in some finite vector space).
Second, for every positive integer
n
, there exist an integer
r=r(n)
, and for every type
t\in\mathcal{T}
there is an
h
-level conditionally linear map (CLM, Definition
4.13
)
\mathfrak{s}^{t}(n)=\mathfrak{s}^{t}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, such that
\mathcal{S}

encodes
the appropriate typed
h
-level sampling scheme (Definition
4.38
):

1.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(\cdot,{\rm Graph},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, then it outputs the graph
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
in the following way: It provides a list of all the types in
\mathcal{T}
according to some order, and then the adjacency matrix associated to
\mathcal{E}
with respect to the order induced on
\mathcal{T}
.

2.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(\overline{n},{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, then it outputs (the binary encoding of)
r(n)
.

3.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(\overline{n},{\rm Register},{\rm Type},j,\mathtt{x},\cdot)
, then it outputs the
j^{\rm th}
register subspace with respect to the seed
\mathtt{x}
, namely
W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}
(
71
) with respect to
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type}
.

4.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(\overline{n},{\rm Marginal},{\rm Type},j,\cdot,z)
, then it outputs the
j^{\rm th}
prefix of
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type}
’s evaluation of
z
, namely
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type}_{\leq j}(z)
.

5.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(\overline{n},{\rm Evaluate},{\rm Type},j,\mathtt{x},z)
, then it outputs the
j^{\rm th}
-register output of
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type}
evaluated on
z
given the seed
\mathtt{x}
, namely
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type,\mathtt{x}}_{j}(z^{W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}})
. Recall that
\mathfrak{s}^{{\rm Type},\mathtt{x}}_{j}\colon W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}\to W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}
is the linear function which controls the
j^{\rm th}
step in the calculation of
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type}
, given that the calculation up to this point produced
\mathtt{x}_{<j}
.

6.

If
\mathcal{S}
gets as input
(\overline{n},{\rm Perpendicular},{\rm Type},j,\mathtt{x},z)
, then it outputs the
j^{\rm th}
-register output of
(\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type})^{\perp}
evaluated on
z
given the seed
\mathtt{x}
, namely
(\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type,\mathtt{x}}_{j})^{\perp}(z^{W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}})
. Recall that the maps
(\mathfrak{s}^{{\rm Type},\mathtt{x}}_{j})^{\perp}\colon W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}\to W^{\mathtt{x}}_{j}
are some fixed linear function whose rows are spanning the subspace perpendicular to the rows of
\mathfrak{s}^{{\rm Type},\mathtt{x}}_{j}
.

Conditions 3. to 6. above are essentially identical to conditions 2. to 5. in Definition Definition
4.29
, except that the “Player” input is replaced by the “Type” input, which may have a bigger range (i.e. the set
\mathcal{T}
).

Definition 4.45

(Typed
h
-level tailored normal form verifier)
.

A
typed
h
-level TNFV
is a quadruple of Turing machines
\mathcal{V}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
,
where
\mathcal{S}
is a typed
h
-level CL sampler as in Definition
4.44
,
\mathcal{A}
is an answer length calculator as in Definition
2.41
,
\mathcal{L}
is a linear constraint processor as in Definition
2.43
, and
\mathcal{D}
is the canonical decider as in Definition
2.45
.

Such a typed normal form verifier is said
\lambda
-bounded
, for a positive integer
\lambda
, if

•

The running times (Definition
2.36
) of
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{L}
are all bounded by
n^{\lambda}
, namely

\forall\overline{n}\in\{0,1\}^{*}\ \colon\ \ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\ ,\ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot)\ ,\ \mathbb{T}(\mathcal{L};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\ \leq\ n^{\lambda}\ .

•

The description length of
\mathcal{V}
is bounded by
\lambda
, namely
|\mathcal{V}|\leq\lambda
(Definition
2.37
).

Similar to Definition
2.48
, Remark
2.49
and Definition
4.33
, when
\mathcal{V}
is a
\lambda
-bounded tailored typed
h
-level normal form verifier, then there is an associated
n^{\rm th}
game to it for every
n\geq 2
. Furthermore, the
n^{\rm th}
game of such a normal form verifier is
well defined
, even if it is not
\lambda
-bounded, if the normal form verifier satisfies the following conditions:

•

\mathcal{A}(\overline{n},(t,\mathtt{x}),\kappa)
halts whenever
t\in\mathcal{T}
, where
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
is the appropriate decoding of
\mathcal{S}(\cdot,{\rm Graph},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
,
\mathtt{x}
is of length
r(n)=\mathcal{S}(\overline{n},{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, and
\kappa\in\{\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{L}\}
.

•

\mathcal{L}(\overline{n},(t,\mathtt{x}),(t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}),a^{\mathfrak{R}},b^{\mathfrak{R}})
halts whenever
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
,
\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y}
are of length
r(n)
, and
a^{\mathfrak{R}}
and
b^{\mathfrak{R}}
are of length
|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\mathfrak{R}))|
and
|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(\overline{n},\mathtt{y},\mathfrak{R}))|
respectively.

Note that by assuming
\mathcal{S}
is a typed
h
-level CL sampler (Definition
4.44
), we are guaranteed that it behaves well, in particular it always halts (on relevant inputs), and there is a type graph and associated CLMs underlying it. So, no additional assumptions on
\mathcal{S}
are needed.

Claim 4.46

(Algorithmic detyping of normal form verifiers)
.

There exists a Turing machine
\mathsf{DeType}_{h}
that takes as input a
typed

h
-level TNFV
\mathcal{V}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
and outputs a (
non-typed
)
(h+2)
-level TNFV

\mathsf{DeType}_{h}(\mathcal{V})=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},\mathcal{D})

such that:

•

(Combinatorial DeTyping) For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\mathcal{V}_{n}
is well defined (Definition
4.45
), then
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}
is well defined (Definition
4.33
) and satisfies
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}=\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathcal{V}_{n})
with respect to the underlying type graph
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
decoded from
\mathcal{S}(\cdot,{\rm Graph},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
.

•

(Sampler properties) The
(h+2)
-level sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
runs in time which is polynomial (with constants that may depend on
h
) in:

–

the number of types
|\mathcal{T}|
, where
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
is the type graph decoded from
\mathcal{S}(\cdot,{\rm Graph},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot).

Moreover,
\mathsf{DeType}_{h}
calculates the description of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
in polynomial time from the description of
\mathcal{S}
, and in particular
|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|=\poly_{h}(|\mathcal{S}|)
.

•

(Answer length properties) The output answer length calculator
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\kappa)
runs in time which is polynomial (with constants that may depend on
h
) in:

–

the number of types
|\mathcal{T}|
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot);

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot)
.

Moreover,
\mathsf{DeType}_{h}
calculates the description of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
in polynomial time in that of
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{A}
; in particular,
|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}|=\poly_{h}(|\mathcal{S}|,|\mathcal{A}|)
.

•

(Linear constraints processor properties) The ouput linear constraints processor TM
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
runs in time polynomial (with constants that may depend on
h
) in:

–

the number of types
|\mathcal{T}|
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot);

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot)
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{L};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
.

Moreover,
\mathsf{DeType}_{h}
calculates the description of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is polynomial time from the descriptions of
\mathcal{S},

\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{L}
; in particular
|\mathcal{L}^{\prime}|=\poly_{h}(|\mathcal{S}|,|\mathcal{A}|,|\mathcal{L}|)
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we use the notation
\textrm{enc}(t)=({\bf 1}_{t},\sum_{t^{\prime}\sim t}{\bf 1}_{t^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
from Definition
4.40
.
Let us describe the sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, answer length calculator
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and linear constraints processor
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathsf{DeType}_{h}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
.

The Sampler:

We start with a detailed description of the operations of the detyped sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. The operation of the sampler follows Definition
4.40
. The details of the implementation are straightforward, but we include them for completeness.

1.

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{n},{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
runs as follows: First, it calls
\mathcal{S}(\cdot,{\rm Graph},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
to extract the set of types
\mathcal{T}
, and thus the size of this set. Then, it calls
\mathcal{S}(\overline{n},{\rm dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
to extract
r(n)
. Finally, it outputs
r^{\prime}(n)=4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
.

2.

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{n},{\rm Register},{\rm Player},j,\mathtt{x},\cdot)
runs as follows:
First, it runs as
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{n},{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
— which was defined in the previous clause — to extract the type set
\mathcal{T}
, the edge set
\mathcal{E}
, and the dimension
r^{\prime}(n)=4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
. Then, it checks that
\mathtt{x}
is a bit string of length
4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
and that
1\leq j\leq h+2
, and outputs an
\mathfrak{error}
sign otherwise.

Now, if
{\rm Player}=A
and
j=1
, then it outputs a bit string of length
4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
whose first
2|\mathcal{T}|
entries are
1
and the rest are
0
, namely

(\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}{\bf 1}_{t},\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}{\bf 1}_{t},\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{0})\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}\ .

Recall that such a bit string is interpreted as the registers which define
W^{\mathtt{x}}_{1}
with respect to the CLM
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
, and in this case it means that regardless of
\mathtt{x}
, this space consists of the first and second copies of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
.

If
{\rm Player}=A
and
j=2
, then it outputs a bit string of length
4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
whose first
2|\mathcal{T}|
entries are
0
, the following
2|\mathcal{T}|
bits are
1
, and the rest are
0
, namely

(\vec{0},\vec{0},\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}{\bf 1}_{t},\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}{\bf 1}_{t},\vec{0})\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}\ .

This means that regardless of
\mathtt{x}
, the register subspace
W^{\mathtt{x}}_{2}
with respect to the CLM
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
is always spanned by the third and fourth copies of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
.

If
{\rm Player}=A
and
j\geq 3
, then it reads the first
4|\mathcal{T}|
bits of
\mathtt{x}
. If there is no type
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that these bits are equal to
(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t})
, then it splits into two cases — if
j=3
, then it outputs a bit string of length
4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
whose first
4|\mathcal{T}|
entries are
0
, and the rest are
1
, namely

(\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{1})\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}\ .

This is interpreted as
W^{3}_{\mathtt{x}}
being a copy of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
. And, if
j>3
, then it outputs a bit string of length
4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
consisting of only zeros, namely
\vec{0}\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
. This is interpreted as
W^{j}_{\mathtt{x}}=\{\vec{0}\}
being the trivial space for any
3<j\leq h+2
.

Otherwise, there is a type
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4\mathcal{T}}}=(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t})
. In this case,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
calls

\mathcal{S}(n,{\rm Register},t,j-2,\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}},\cdot)\ ,

whose output is denoted by
\vec{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
; then, it outputs a bit string of length
4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
whose first
4|\mathcal{T}|
entries are
0
, and the rest are
\vec{i}
, namely

(\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{i})\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}\ .

This is interpreted as
W^{j}_{\mathtt{x}}
with respect to
\mathfrak{s}^{A}
being
\{(\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{0},\vec{0})\}\times W^{j-2}_{\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}}}
, where
W^{j-2}_{\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}}}
is the
(j-2)^{\rm th}
-register subspace given the seed
\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}}
with respect to the CLM
\mathfrak{s}^{t}
.

The case where
{\rm Player}=B
is similar, and we omit its description.

3.

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{n},{\rm Marginal},{\rm Player},j,\cdot,z)
runs as follows: First, it runs
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{n},{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
— which was defined in the first clause — to extract the type set
\mathcal{T}
, the edge set
\mathcal{E}
, and the dimension
r^{\prime}(n)=4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
. Then, it checks that
z
is a bit string of length
4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)
and that
1\leq j\leq h+2
, and returns an
\mathfrak{error}
sign otherwise.

Now, if
{\rm Player}=A
and
j=1
, then
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
outputs the first
2|\mathcal{T}|
bits of
z
. If
{\rm Player}=A
and
j=2
, then
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
reads the first
2|\mathcal{T}|
bits of
z
. If
(z_{1},...,z_{2|\mathcal{T}|})=\textrm{enc}(t)
for some
t\in\mathcal{T}
, then it zeroes out all coordinates in the third copy of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
, and all coordinates but the
t^{\rm th}
one in the fourth copy of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
; otherwise, it zeroes out the third and fourth copy of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
completely. If
{\rm Player}=A
and
j\geq 3
, then it does the first two steps as described above, resulting in a vector
\mathtt{x}
in
(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}
; if there is no
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathtt{x}=(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t})
, then it zeros out all coordinates of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
, resulting in
(\mathtt{x},\vec{0})
. Otherwise, there is some
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathtt{x}=(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t})
, in which case it calls
\mathcal{S}(n,{\rm Marginal},t,j-2,\cdot,z|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}})
, whose output we denote by
\mathtt{x}^{\prime}
, and it outputs
(\mathtt{x},\mathtt{x}^{\prime})\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}})^{4}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
.

Again, the case
{\rm Player}=B
is similar, and we omit it.

4.

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{n},{\rm Evaluate},{\rm Player},j,\mathtt{x},z)
runs as follows: If
j=1
, then it runs
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{n},{\rm Marginal},{\rm Player},j,\cdot,z)
as defined in the previous clause. If
{\rm Player}=A
and
j=2
, then it checks whether the restriction of
\mathtt{x}
to the first and second copies of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
agrees with
\textrm{enc}(t)
for some
t\in\mathcal{T}
; if it does, then it outputs the vector
(\vec{0},\mathtt{x}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
, where
\mathtt{x}^{\prime}
is the vector whose all coordinates are zero except for the
t^{\rm th}
coordinate, which is the
t^{\rm th}
coordinate in the fourth copy of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
in
z
; otherwise, it outputs
\vec{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
. If
{\rm Player}=A
and
j\geq 3
, it first checks whether the restriction of
\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4|\mathcal{T}|}}=(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t})
for some
t\in\mathcal{T}
; if so, it outputs the same output as
\mathcal{S}(\overline{n},{\rm Evaluate},t,j-2,\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}},z|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}})
; otherwise, if
j=3
, then it outputs
\vec{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
, and if
j>3
it outputs the empty string. For
{\rm Player}=B
, it acts similarly with the first and second copies of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\mathcal{T}}
swapping roles with the third and fourth copies.

5.

There is a
canonical
way of extracting the perpendicular action out of the others. See Clause 6 on page 94 of
[
JNV+21
]
, which explains how step 3c in Figure 10 is implemented.

Before describing the rest of the TMs, we note that indeed the sampler satisfies the conditions of the proof:

For the first condition, which says that this transformed sampler is indeed the detyping transformation on the combinatorial level, we leave for the reader to compare this algorithm to the description in Definition
4.40
.

For efficient runtime, note that all the operations are either calling the original sampler on the same index
n
namely
\mathcal{S}(\overline{n},\cdot,\ldots,\cdot)
, or a previous subroutine which was already defined (again on the same index
n
), or is some polynomial time operation on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)+4|\mathcal{T}|}
which translates to
\poly(|\mathcal{T}|,r(n))
number of operations. As both
|\mathcal{T}|
and
r(n)
are bounded by
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
(Remark
4.31
), the runtime bound is deduced.

To deduce that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
can be described in length that is polynomial in that of
\mathcal{S}
, note that the (constant length) natural language description we provided above can be translated to a constant length code in some programming language (or more precisely, a formal description of a TM according to the encoding fixed in Section
2.5.1
).
Therefore, the description length of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is some constant, up to the appending of the description of
\mathcal{S}
(for it to run the appropriate subroutines).
As the exact effect of appending
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
on the level of the description of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
depends on the specific choice of encodings of TMs, we use
Item

(3)
of Fact
2.32
, and deduce that the description of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
can be calculated from
\mathcal{S}
in polynomial time and thus
|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|=\poly_{h}(|\mathcal{S}|)
.
63
63

63

Actually, the description is fixed up to appending
\mathcal{S}
and
h
, which means the dependence is
\poly(\log h,|\mathcal{S}|)
. This is better than
\poly_{h}(|\mathcal{S}|)
, but we do not need this better bound.

The Answer length calculator:

Recall that the input to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is expected to be
(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\kappa)
, where
\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)}
and
\kappa\in\{\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{L}\}
. On the other hand,
\mathcal{A}
is expecting an input of the form
(\overline{n},(t,\mathtt{y}),\kappa)
, where
t\in\mathcal{T}
and
\mathtt{y}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
.

So,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\kappa)
runs as follows: It calls
\mathcal{S}
to extract
\mathcal{T}
and
r(n)
, and then if the conditions on the inputs are not satisfied, it outputs an
\mathfrak{error}
sign. Otherwise, it checks whether there is a
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4|\mathcal{T}|}}=(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t})
or
\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4|\mathcal{T}|}}=(\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t},\textrm{enc}(t))
. If not, it outputs the empty string (which is the unary representation of
0
). If there is such a
t
, it provides the output of
\mathcal{A}(\overline{n},(t,\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}}),\kappa)
.

Again, it is straightforward to check that this induces a length function which is compatible with the description of
\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{G})
in Definition
4.40
. For description length, again the above description is constant up to fixing the appropriate inputs to
\mathsf{DeType}_{h}
. Lastly,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
calls
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{A}
, and does some polynomial time manipulations on vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)}
. Hence, it runs in time polynomial in the above.

The Linear constraints processor:

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y},a^{\mathfrak{R}},b^{\mathfrak{R}})
runs as follows. First, it calls
\mathcal{S}
to extract
\mathcal{T}
and
r(n)
. Then, it checks that
\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4|\mathcal{T}|+r(n)}
, and if not outputs the single constraint
\{\mathsf{J}\}
. Otherwise, it calls
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\mathfrak{R})
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\overline{n},\mathtt{y},\mathfrak{R})
and compares the lengths of
a^{\mathfrak{R}}
and
b^{\mathfrak{R}}
to their decoded outputs respectively. If they do not match, it outputs the single constraint
\{\mathsf{J}\}
. Finally, if the input passed all the above checks, then
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
checks the following: If there are no
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathtt{x}=(\textrm{enc}(t),\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t})
and
\mathtt{y}=(\vec{0},{\bf 1}_{t^{\prime}},\textrm{enc}(t^{\prime}))
, then it outputs the empty string (interpreted as no constraints, which is immediate acceptance). Otherwise, it returns the output of
\mathcal{L}(\overline{n},(t,\mathtt{x}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}}),(t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}}),a^{\mathfrak{R}},b^{\mathfrak{R}})
.

As for the sampler and answer length calculator, checking that this
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
satisfies what we need is straightforward, and we leave it to the reader. This finishes the proof.
∎

4.5.4
Padding

The idea of padding is enlarging the length of answers artificially in a way that
essentially
does not change the game. There are many ways of doing that, and the following is a simple version which is
restriction free
— namely, we enlarge the answer length and do not require the appended bits to satisfy any requirements.

Definition 4.47

(Restriction free combinatorial padding)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a tailored game, and
\Lambda
a positive integer. The tailored game

\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}=\mathfrak{Padding}(\mathfrak{G},\Lambda)

has the same underlying graph as
\mathfrak{G}
, and the same edge sampling distribution
\mu
. For every
vertex
\mathtt{x}\in V
, both the readable length and unreadable length of
\mathtt{x}
are defined to be
\Lambda
, namely
\widetilde{\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}}(\mathtt{x})=\widetilde{\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}}(\mathtt{x})=\Lambda
. For the controlled linear constraints function
\widetilde{L_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}}
:

•

If the readable or unreadable length of either
\mathtt{x}
or
\mathtt{y}
are larger than
\Lambda
, namely

\Lambda<\max\{\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathtt{x}),\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathtt{y}),\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}(\mathtt{x}),\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}(\mathtt{y})\}\ ,

then
\widetilde{L_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}}
outputs no constraints regardless of what
\gamma^{\mathfrak{R}}
is (which translates to automatic acceptance).

•

Otherwise, for every
\mathtt{z}\in V
we let
\widetilde{S_{\mathtt{z}}^{\mathfrak{R}}}=S_{\mathtt{z}}^{\mathfrak{R}}\sqcup T^{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathtt{z}}
, where
S^{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathtt{z}}
is the original formal set of generators at
\mathtt{z}
and
T^{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathtt{z}}
is a set of
\Lambda-\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathtt{z})
many additional variables (and similarly for
\widetilde{S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{z}}}
). Namely,

\widetilde{S^{\cdot}_{\cdot}}={S^{\cdot}_{\cdot}}\sqcup{T^{\cdot}_{\cdot}}

where the subscripts can be
\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y}
or
\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}
(which indicates union of
\mathtt{x}
and
\mathtt{y}
variables) and the superscripts can be
\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{L}
or none (which indicates the union of readable and unreadable variables).
Recall that for
\gamma^{\mathfrak{R}}\colon S^{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
, the output of
L_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}(\gamma^{\mathfrak{R}})
is a collection of indicators on the set
S_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}\sqcup\{\mathsf{J}\}
, representing linear constraints that should be checked on
\gamma\colon S_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
So, for
\widetilde{\gamma^{\mathfrak{R}}}\colon\widetilde{S^{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
, letting
\gamma^{\mathfrak{R}}=\widetilde{\gamma^{\mathfrak{R}}}|_{S_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}^{\mathfrak{R}}}
, we can define
\widetilde{L_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}}(\widetilde{\gamma^{\mathfrak{R}}})
to be the extension by zeros of the outputs of
L_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}(\gamma^{\mathfrak{R}})
to the
T_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}
variables. Namely, for every
c\colon S_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}\sqcup\{\mathsf{J}\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
in the output of
L_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}(\gamma^{\mathfrak{R}})
, we let
\tilde{c}\colon\widetilde{S_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}}\sqcup\{\mathsf{J}\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
be defined by

\tilde{c}(\mathsf{X})=\begin{cases}c(\mathsf{X})&\mathsf{X}\in S_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}\sqcup\{\mathsf{J}\}\ ,\\
0&\mathsf{X}\in T_{\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}}\ .\end{cases}

On the combinatorial level, the padded game
\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
samples an edge from the original graph (according to the same distribution), and as long as
\Lambda
is large enough, it disregards the added variables
T
and plays the original game
\mathfrak{G}
only according to the assignments to
S
.

The following is a straightforward fact to check.

Fact 4.48
.

Assume
\Lambda\geq\max(\ell^{\mathfrak{R}},\ell^{\mathfrak{L}})
. Then,

•

(Completeness) If
\mathfrak{G}
has a perfect
\mathsf{ZPC}
strategy, then so does
\mathfrak{Padding}(\mathfrak{G},\Lambda)
.

•

(Soundness and entanglement) If
\mathfrak{Padding}(\mathfrak{G},\Lambda)
has a value
1-\varepsilon
strategy, then so does
\mathfrak{G}
, and furthermore

\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{Padding}(\mathfrak{G},\Lambda),1-\varepsilon)=\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{G},1-\varepsilon)\ .

Claim 4.49
.

There is a polynomial-time TM
\mathsf{Padding}
that takes as input a tailored
h
-level normal form verifier
\mathcal{V}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
and a
1
-input TM
\Lambda
, and outputs a new tailored
h
-level normal form verifier
\mathsf{Padding}(\mathcal{V},\Lambda)=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}^{\Lambda},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},\mathcal{D})
satisfying:

•

(Combinatorial Padding) For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\mathcal{V}_{n}
is well defined (Definition
4.33
), then
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}
is well defined, and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}=\mathfrak{Padding}(\mathcal{V}_{n},|\Lambda(n)|)
, where
|\cdot|
is the length of words function.
64
64

64

This is the same as thinking of the output of
\Lambda(n)
as representing a natural number in unary.

•

(Sampler properties) The output sampler is the same as the original one, and thus its running time and description lengths stay the same.

•

(Answer length properties) The output answer length TM
\mathcal{A}^{\Lambda}
depends only on
\Lambda
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{A}^{\Lambda}(n,\cdot,\cdot)
runs in time which is linear in
\mathbb{T}(\Lambda;n)
. Finally, the description length of
\mathcal{A}^{\Lambda}
is linear in that of
\Lambda
.

•

(Linear constraints processor properties) The output linear constraints processor
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
runs in time which is polynomial in:

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\Lambda;\overline{n})
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot);

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot)
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{L};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
.

Moreover, the description length of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is linear in that of
\Lambda,\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

The Sampler:
We keep
\mathcal{S}
as the sampler. So, running time and description length stay the same.

The answer length calculator:
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\Lambda}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\kappa)=\textrm{enc}(\Lambda(n))
(Definition
2.34
), regardless of
\mathtt{x}
or
\kappa
.
Hence,
|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}^{\Lambda}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\kappa))|=|\Lambda(n)|
, as is needed for
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}
to be equal to
\mathfrak{Padding}(\mathcal{V}_{n},|\Lambda(n)|)
. It is immediate that the running time and description length are linear in
\Lambda
’s.

The linear constraints processor:

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y},a^{\mathfrak{R}},b^{\mathfrak{R}})
runs as follows.
First it calls
\mathcal{S}(\overline{n},{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
to retrieve
r(n)
and checks that
\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r(n)}
; if not, it outputs
\{\mathsf{J}\}
65
65

65

By outputting
\{\mathsf{J}\}
, we mean the encoding (as in Definition
2.43
) of the bit string of length
4|\Lambda(n)|+1
where all of its bits are zero except the last one which is
1
.
(which is instant rejection); if they do satisfy this condition, then it checks whether
|a^{\mathfrak{R}}|=|b^{\mathfrak{R}}|=|\Lambda(n)|
; if not, it outputs
\{\mathsf{J}\}
; otherwise, the inputs are well structured and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
can proceed.

Now,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
calls

\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}_{a}=|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\mathfrak{R}))|\ ,\ \ell^{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}=|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(\overline{n},\mathtt{y},\mathfrak{R}))|\ ,\ \ell^{\mathfrak{L}}_{a}=|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\mathfrak{L}))|\quad{\rm and}\quad\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}_{b}=|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(\overline{n},\mathtt{y},\mathfrak{L}))|\ .

If
|\Lambda(n)|
is strictly smaller than either of the lengths of these outputs, then
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
outputs the empty string (i.e., no constraints). Otherwise, let
a_{0}^{\mathfrak{R}}
be the restriction of
a^{\mathfrak{R}}
to its first
|\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}_{a}|
bits and
b_{0}^{\mathfrak{R}}
be the restriction of
b^{\mathfrak{R}}
to its first
|\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}|
bits.
Then,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
calls
\mathcal{L}(\overline{n},\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y},a^{\mathfrak{R}}_{0},b^{\mathfrak{R}}_{0})
and gets as output a bit string. If this bit string is not (the encoding) of bit strings
(c^{1},...,c^{k})
, where every
c^{i}
is of length
|\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}_{a}|+|\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}|+|\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}_{a}|+|\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}_{b}|+1
, then it outputs
\{\mathsf{J}\}
. Otherwise, it does the following operation on each
c^{i}
: First, it splits it to
5
bit strings
c^{i}_{a,\mathfrak{R}},c^{i}_{b,\mathfrak{R}},c^{i}_{a,\mathfrak{L}},c^{i}_{b,\mathfrak{L}},c^{i}_{\mathsf{J}}
of lengths
\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}_{a},\ell^{\mathfrak{R}}_{b},\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}_{a},\ell^{\mathfrak{L}}_{b}
and
1
respectively. Then, it appends each of
c^{i}_{a,\mathfrak{R}},c^{i}_{b,\mathfrak{R}},c^{i}_{a,\mathfrak{L}},c^{i}_{b,\mathfrak{L}}
with zeros until they are of length
|\Lambda(n)|
— we denote the resulting strings by
\tilde{c}^{i}_{\cdot,\cdot}
. Finally, the bit string
\tilde{c}^{i}
of length
4|\Lambda(n)|+1
is defined to be the concatenation of
\tilde{c}^{i}_{a,\mathfrak{R}},\tilde{c}^{i}_{b,\mathfrak{R}},\tilde{c}^{i}_{a,\mathfrak{L}},\tilde{c}^{i}_{b,\mathfrak{L}}
and
c^{i}_{\mathsf{J}}
.
After this operation was done for each string
c^{i}
, resulting with new strings
\tilde{c}^{i}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
outputs (the encoding of)
(\tilde{c}^{1},...,\tilde{c}^{k})
.

The description is again just the above finite one, with the inputs
\mathcal{V}
and
\Lambda
fixed. Hence, by
Item

(3)
in Fact
2.32
, the description can be calculated in polynomial time from them and is thus of polynomial length. For running time,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
either calls
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}
or
\mathcal{L}
, or is applying polynomial time operations on bit strings of length at most
O(|\Lambda(n)|)
— where
|\Lambda(n)|
is a quantity smaller than the running time of
\Lambda(n)
— or bit strings of length at most
r(n)
. Recall that
r(n)
is bounded by
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};\overline{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
by Remark
4.31
, which explains the time bounds of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
.
∎

4.6
Proving the main theorem of Question Reduction: Theorem
4.36

Let
\mathcal{V}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
be a tailored
h
-level normal form verifier, and
\lambda
a positive integer. The goal is to describe the question reduced verifier
\mathsf{QuestionReduction}_{h}(\mathcal{V},\lambda)=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}_{\textsc{QR}},\mathcal{A}^{\lambda}_{\textsc{QR}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},\mathcal{D})
which proves the theorem. The idea is, under the assumption that
\mathcal{V}
is
\lambda
-bounded, to first choose for every
n
: an appropriate integer
k
which will be larger than the dimension of the CLMs used in
\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}}
, namely larger than
\mathcal{S}(2^{n},{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
; an appropriate
\Lambda
which will be larger than the lengths used in
\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}}
, namely larger than every possible
\mathcal{A}(2^{n},\cdot,\cdot)
; an appropriate
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
that would have size a power of
2
, and induce a good error correcting code with some predetermined parameters. After these choices are made, the goal of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
is for its
n^{\rm th}
game to be, combinatorially,

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}=\mathfrak{DeType}(\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathfrak{Padding}(\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}},\Lambda),k,\mathscr{B})\;.

We already described how to detype and to pad on the level of verifiers in Claims
4.46
and
4.49
. So, we are left to describe a Turing machine that assumes the input is already padded, and outputs a
typed
normal form verifier that implements combinatorial question reduction. In the next claim, we let
\mathscr{B}
be a (fixed) TM that takes as input a bit string
\mathtt{x}
of length
m
and outputs a list of
2\cdot 2^{m}
vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{m}}
that induces an encoding matrix whose associated code has normalized distance
\delta
for some universal constant
\delta>0
, and such that
\mathbb{T}(\mathscr{B};\mathtt{x})=2^{O(|\mathtt{x}|)}
(the existence of such a TM
\mathscr{B}
and such a universal constant
\delta
is guaranteed by Fact
3.72
).

Claim 4.50
.

There is a polynomial time TM
\mathsf{TypedQuestionReduction}_{h}
that takes two inputs — an
h
-level normal form verifier
\mathcal{V}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D})
; a
1
-input TM
\mathcal{K}
— and outputs a typed
1
-level normal form verifier

\mathsf{TypedQuestionReduction}_{h}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{K})=\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}=(\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{D})

such that

•

(Combinatorial Question Reduction) For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}}
is well defined (Definition
4.33
), and there is a function
\Delta\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
|{{\rm dec}}(\mathcal{A}(2^{n},\mathtt{x},\kappa))|=\Delta(n)
regardless of
\mathtt{x}
and
\kappa
, and
2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}
is larger than

\mathcal{S}(2^{n},{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\;,

then
\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
is well defined (Definition
4.45
), and

\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{n}=\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}},2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|},\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{K}(n)))\ .

•

(Sampler properties) The sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}
depends only on
\mathcal{K}
(and
h
), but not on
\mathcal{V}
. Furthermore, it runs in time which is polynomial in that of
\mathcal{K}
.
66
66

66

Here, the constants depend on
h
, namely this is
\poly_{h}(\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{K};n))
.
Finally, its description can be calculated from that of
\mathcal{K}
in polynomial time, which means in particular
|\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}|=\poly_{h}(|\mathcal{K}|)
.

•

(Answer length calculator properties) The TM
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
depends only on
\mathcal{K}
and on
\mathcal{A}
, and not on
\mathcal{S}
or
\mathcal{L}
. In addition, it runs in time

\mathbb{T}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}};n,\cdot,\cdot)=\poly_{h}(2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|},\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{K};n),\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};2^{n},\cdot,\cdot))\ .

Finally, its description can be calculated in polynomial time from the relevant inputs, and in particular
|\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}|\leq\poly_{h}(|\mathcal{K}|,|\mathcal{A}|)
.

•

(Linear constraints processor properties)
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
runs in time which is polynomial in:

–

the integer
2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{K};n)
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{S};2^{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};2^{n},\cdot,\cdot)
;

–

the running time
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{L};2^{n},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
;

Furthermore, its description can be calculated from
\mathcal{K},\Lambda,\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{L}
in polynomial time, and in particular
|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}|\leq\poly_{h}(|\mathcal{K}|,|\Lambda,|\mathcal{S}|,|\mathcal{L}|)
.

Proof.

The typed CL sampler:
Recall Example
4.39
, and specifically that we aim to define a typed
1
-level sampler. This means that, in particular, in the expected input
(n,{\rm Action},{\rm Type},j,\mathtt{x},z)
, we can assume that
j=1
always.

1.

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,{\rm Graph},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
outputs the list

\begin{split}\forall 1\leq j\leq h\ \colon\ \ &\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{j}\ ,\ \mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{j}\ ,\\
&\mathtt{Intro}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Intro}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{B}\ ,\\
&\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}\ ,\ \mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}\ ,\ \mathtt{X}\ ,\ \mathtt{Z}\ ,\ \mathtt{First}\ ,\ \mathtt{Second}\ ,\ \mathtt{Both}\ ,\\
\forall 1\leq i\leq 3\ ,\ 1\leq j\leq 3\ \colon\ \ &\mathtt{var}_{ij}\ ,\ \mathtt{row}_{i}\ ,\ \mathtt{col}_{j}\;,\end{split}

followed by the adjacency matrix of the graph depicted in Figure
14
.

2.

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(n,{\rm Dimension},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
calls
\mathcal{K}(n)
and outputs
2|\mathcal{K}(n)|+2=2\log|\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{K}(n))|
.

3.

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(n,{\rm Register},{\rm Type},1,\mathtt{x},\cdot)
outputs a bit string consisiting of only
1
’s of length
2|\mathcal{K}(n)|+2
, which indicates that the whole space is the first (and only) register subspace of
\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type}
— note that this requires it to first call
\mathcal{K}(n)
as a subroutine.

4.

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(n,{\rm Marginal},{\rm Type},1,\cdot,z)
runs as follows:

•

If
{\rm Type}
is one of

\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{j}\ ,\ \mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{j}\ ,\ \ \mathtt{Intro}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Intro}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}\ ,\ \mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}\ ,

then it zeroes out
z
and outputs
\vec{0}
(which is a concatenation of
2|\mathcal{K}(n)|+2
zeros in this case).

•

If
{\rm Type}
is one of

\mathtt{row}_{i}\ ,\ \mathtt{col}_{j}\ ,\ \mathtt{var}_{ij}\ ,\ \mathtt{First}\ ,\ \mathtt{Second}\ ,\ \mathtt{Both}\ ,

then it acts as the identity on
z
, namely outputs
z
.

•

If
{\rm Type}
is either
\mathtt{X}
or
\mathtt{Z}
, let us split
z
into
z_{1},z_{2}
, where
z_{1}
is the first
|\mathcal{K}(n)|+1
bits of
z
and
z_{2}
are its last
|\mathcal{K}(n)|+1
bits. Then, given
{\rm Type}=\mathtt{X}
, it outputs
(z_{1},\vec{0})
, and given
{\rm Type}=\mathtt{Z}
, it outputs
(\vec{0},z_{2})
(here
\vec{0}
is a concatenation of
|\mathcal{K}(n)|+1
zeros).

5.

As, again, we can assume
j=1
,
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(n,{\rm Evaluate},{\rm Type},1,\mathtt{x},z)
runs exactly the same as
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(n,{\rm Marginal},{\rm Type},1,\cdot,z)
.

6.

There is a
canonical
way of extracting the perpendicular action out of the others. See Clause 6 on page 94 of
[
JNV+21
]
, which explains how step 3c in Figure 10 is implemented. Albeit, in this case it is straightforward what the perpendicular maps are.
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(n,{\rm Perpendicular},{\rm Type},1,\mathtt{x},z)
runs as follows:

•

If
{\rm Type}
is one of

\mathtt{Hide}_{A}^{j}\ ,\ \mathtt{Hide}_{B}^{j}\ ,\ \ \mathtt{Intro}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Intro}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Read}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{A}\ ,\ \mathtt{Sample}_{B}\ ,\ \mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{Z}}\ ,\ \mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}}\ ,

then
(\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type})^{\perp}
should act as the identity, and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}
outputs
z
.

•

If
{\rm Type}
is one of

\mathtt{row}_{i}\ ,\ \mathtt{col}_{j}\ ,\ \mathtt{var}_{ij}\ ,\ \mathtt{First}\ ,\ \mathtt{Second}\ ,\ \mathtt{Both}\ ,

then
(\mathfrak{s}^{\rm Type})^{\perp}
should act as the zero map, and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}
outputs
\vec{0}
.

•

Finally,
(\mathfrak{s}^{\mathtt{X}})^{\perp}(z)=(\vec{0},z_{2})
and
(\mathfrak{s}^{\mathtt{Z}})^{\perp}(z)=(z_{1},\vec{0})
, which means
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}
outputs
(\vec{0},z_{2})
in case
{\rm Type}=\mathtt{X}
and
(z_{1},\vec{0})
in case
{\rm Type}=\mathtt{Z}
.

We verify the required properties of the typed sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}
. Note that the above description is constant, and the only thing that actually needs to be appended is the description length of
\mathcal{K}
. By
Item

(3)
of Fact
2.32
, this shows that the description of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}
can be calculated from that of
\mathcal{K}
in polynomial time, which in particular implies the description length bound. For runtime, note that all the operations done by
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}
are either writing down the type graph (which takes
\poly(h)
-time), calls to
\mathcal{K}(n)
, or manipulations of vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2|\mathcal{K}(n)|+2}
— that take time at most
\poly(|\mathcal{K}(n)|)
, which is polynomial in the running time of
\mathcal{K}
. All in all, the running time is polynomial in that of
h
and
\mathcal{K}
.

The Answer length calculator:

Recall that the readable and unreadable lengths of a vertex in
\mathfrak{QueRed}
(Section
4.4
) depend only on its type.
Hence,
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(n,(t,\mathtt{x}),\kappa)
runs as follows: First, it calls
\mathcal{K}(n)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,{\rm Graph},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
to retrieve the type set
\mathcal{T}
underlying the typed
1
-level CL sampling scheme. If
t\notin\mathcal{T}
or
\mathtt{x}\notin\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2|\mathcal{K}(n)|+2}
or
\kappa\notin\{\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{L}\}
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
outputs an
\mathfrak{error}
sign. Otherwise, it lets
\Delta(n)=|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(2^{n},0,\mathfrak{R}))|
,
67
67

67

Here,
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
will work as expected only if
\mathcal{A}
is indeed padded and disregards its second and third inputs altogether.
and follows the table:

We verify the required properties of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
. The above description is constant, up to appending the descriptions of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{A}
.
For running time, note that:

•

it calls
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,{\rm Graph},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
which takes
\poly(h)
time;

•

it calls
\mathcal{K}(n)
which takes
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{K};n)
time;

•

it verifies certain properties on bit strings of length
O(|\mathcal{K}(n)|)
, which takes
\poly(|\mathcal{K}(n)|)
time;

•

it calls
\mathcal{A}(2^{n},\cdot,\cdot)
which takes
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};2^{n},\cdot,\cdot)
time, and its output is of length
\Delta(n)
which by definition is smaller or equal to
\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};2^{n},\cdot,\cdot)
;

•

it outputs bit strings of length
O(2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}+\Delta(n))
, which takes
\poly(2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|},\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};2^{n},\cdot,\cdot))
time.

All in all, it runs in time which is
\poly(2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|},\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{K};n),\mathbb{T}(\mathcal{A};2^{n},\cdot,\cdot))
, as claimed.

The Linear constraints processor:

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(n,(t,\mathtt{y}),(t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}^{\prime}),a^{\mathfrak{R}},b^{\mathfrak{R}})
runs as follows.
First it calls
\mathcal{K}(n)
, and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,{\rm Graph},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
— to get the type graph
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E})
. If
tt^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{E}
or
\mathtt{y},\mathtt{y}^{\prime}\notin\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2|\mathcal{K}(n)|+2}
, then it outputs
\mathfrak{error}
(note that in this case, the canonical decider will reject as this sign is not a proper encoding of a sequence of bit strings).
Then, it checks that
|a^{\mathfrak{R}}|=|{\rm dec}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(n,(t,\mathtt{y}),\mathfrak{R}))|
and that
|b^{\mathfrak{R}}|=|{\rm dec}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(n,(t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{R}))|
, and outputs
\mathfrak{error}
otherwise. Given that the input was well structured, it runs
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{K}(n))
, which outputs a sequence
\mathscr{B}
of
2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|+1}
-many vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}
— this can be thought of as a matrix over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
with
2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}
columns and
2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|+1}
rows — and thus the elements of
\mathscr{B}
(which are the rows of the aforementioned matrix) can be parameterized by vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|+1}
. Finally, it recovers the value
\Delta(n)=|{\rm dec}(\mathcal{A}(2^{n},0,\mathfrak{R}))|
. Then,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
acts as follows:
68
68

68

The format is the following: Each enumerated clause is some sub graph of the typed graph of
\mathfrak{QueRed}
, which should help navigate the checks more easily. The actual operation of
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
is to check what is the relevant question format, and acting according to the appropriate bullet. We first go over edges from
{\mathfrak{Pauli\ Basis}}_{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}(\mathscr{B})
which was described in Section
3.8.3
, then the single edge from
\mathfrak{Intro}(\mathcal{V}_{2^{n}})
which was described in Section
4.1
, and finally the augmented edges of
\mathfrak{QueRed}(\mathcal{V}_{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}})
described in Section
4.4
.

1.

Pauli Basis Test — Consistency checks of
\mathsf{X}
-variables
:

•

Question format
:
(t,\mathtt{y})=(\mathtt{Pauli}_{\mathds{X}},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}^{\prime})=(\mathtt{X},u,\vec{0})
.

Operation
: The bit string
u\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|+1}
is the index of some vector
w^{u}\in\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}
. Then,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
outputs (the encoding) of the single bit string
(w^{u},1,0)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}+2}
.

Interpretation
: In this case
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{Pauli}_{{\mathds{X}}}}=\{\mathsf{P}\mathsf{X}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}
and
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{X}^{u}}=\{\mathsf{X}^{u}\}
, and the above bit string encodes the linear constraint

\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}w^{u}_{i}\gamma(\mathsf{P}\mathsf{X}^{i})\Big)+\gamma(\mathsf{X}^{u})=0\;.

•

Question format
:
(t,\mathtt{y})=(\mathtt{var}_{11},u,v)\ ,\ (t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}^{\prime})=(\mathtt{X},u,\vec{0})
.

Operation
:
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
outputs (the encoding) of the single bit string
(1,1,0)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{3}
.

Interpretation
: In this case
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{var}_{11}^{u,v}}=\{\mathsf{Var}_{11}^{u,v}\}
and
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{X}^{u}}=\{\mathsf{X}^{u}\}
, and the above bit string encodes the linear constraint

\gamma(\mathsf{Var}_{11}^{u,v})+\gamma(\mathsf{X}^{u})=0\;.

•

Question format
:
(t,\mathtt{y})=(\mathtt{First},u,v)\ ,\ (t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}^{\prime})=(\mathtt{X},u,\vec{0})
.

Operation
:
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
outputs (the encoding) of the single bit string
(1,1,0)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{3}
.

Interpretation
: In this case
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{First}^{u,v}}=\{\mathsf{First}^{u,v}\}
and
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{X}^{u}}=\{\mathsf{X}^{u}\}
, and the above bit string encodes the linear constraint

\gamma(\mathsf{First}^{u,v})+\gamma(\mathsf{X}^{u})=0.

2.

\mathsf{Z}
-variables:

3.

Pauli Basis Test — (null-)Commutation game
(Section
3.8.1
):

•

Question format
:
(t,\mathtt{y})=(\mathtt{First},u,v)\ ,\ (t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}^{\prime})=(\mathtt{Both},u,v)
.

Operation
:
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
reads
w^{u},w^{v}\in\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}
. Then, it calculates
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle
. If the result is
1
, it outputs the empty string (which translates to immediate acceptance). Otherwise, it outputs
(1,1,0,0)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4}
.

Interpretation
: In this case
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{First}^{u,v}}=\{\mathsf{First}^{u,v}\}
and
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{Both}^{u,v}}=\{\mathsf{Both}^{u,v}_{1},\mathsf{Both}^{u,v}_{2}\}
. If
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle=1,
then this is a copy of the null-commutation game
\mathfrak{C}_{null}
, which always accepts. Otherwise,
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle=0
and the game is the commutation game
\mathfrak{C}
, in which case the single bit string encodes the linear constraint

\gamma(\mathsf{First}^{u,v})+\gamma(\mathsf{Both}_{1}^{u,v})=0\;.

•

Question format
:
(t,\mathtt{y})=(\mathtt{Second},u,v)\ ,\ (t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}^{\prime})=(\mathtt{Both},u,v)
.

Operation
:
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
reads
w^{u},w^{v}\in\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}
. Then, it calculates
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle
. If the result is
1
, it outputs the empty string (which translates to immediate acceptance). Otherwise, it outputs
(1,0,1,0)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4}
.

Interpretation
: In this case
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{Second}^{u,v}}=\{\mathsf{Second}^{u,v}\}
and
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{Both}^{u,v}}=\{\mathsf{Both}^{u,v}_{1},\mathsf{Both}^{u,v}_{2}\}
. If
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle=1,
then this is a copy of the null-commutation game
\mathfrak{C}_{null}
, which always accepts. Otherwise,
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle=0
and the game is the commutation game
\mathfrak{C}
, in which case the single bit string encodes the linear constraint

\gamma(\mathsf{Second}^{u,v})+\gamma(\mathsf{Both}_{2}^{u,v})=0\;.

4.

Pauli Basis Test — (null-)Anti-Commutation game
(Section
3.8.2
): For every
1\leq i,j\leq 3
,

•

Question format
:
(t,\mathtt{y})=(\mathtt{var}_{ab},u,v)\ ,\ (t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}^{\prime})=(\mathtt{row}_{a},u,v)
.

Operation
:
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
reads
w^{u},w^{v}\in\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}
. Then, it calculates
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle
. If the result is
0
, it outputs the empty string (which translates to immediate acceptance). Otherwise, given that
e_{b}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{3}
is the
b^{\rm th}
vector of the standard basis (i.e., the indicator of
b
),
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
outputs (the encoding) of the two bit strings
(1,e_{b},0),(0,1,1,1,0)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{5}
.
69
69

69

Just as a sanity check, e.g. if
b=1
, we seek the encoding of
11000\sqcup 01110
, which is
0101000000100001010100
according to Definition
2.34
.

Interpretation
: In this case
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{var}_{ab}^{u,v}}=\{\mathsf{Var}_{ab}^{u,v}\}
and
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{row}_{a}^{u,v}}=\{\mathsf{Row}_{a1}^{u,v},\mathsf{Row}^{u,v}_{a2},\mathsf{Row}^{u,v}_{a3}\}
. If
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle=0,
then this is a copy of the null-anti-commutation game
\mathfrak{M}_{null}
, which always accepts. Otherwise,
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle=1
and the game is the anti-commutation game
\mathfrak{M}
— i.e., the magic square game (Example
2.30
) — in which case the two bit strings encode the linear constraints

\gamma(\mathsf{Var}_{ab}^{u,v})+\gamma(\mathsf{Row}_{ab}^{u,v})=0\quad{\rm and}\quad\gamma(\mathsf{Row}_{a1}^{u,v})+\gamma(\mathsf{Row}_{a2}^{u,v})+\gamma(\mathsf{Row}_{a3}^{u,v})=0\;.

Operation
: (This is very similar to the previous case, with rows and columns swapped, and with the sum along columns needing to be

vector of the standard basis,

Interpretation
: In this case

and

. If

then this is a copy of the null-anti-commutation game

, which always accepts. Otherwise,
\langle w^{u},w^{v}\rangle=1

) — in which case the two bit strings encode the linear constraints

5.

Introspection Game
(Section
4.1
):

Question format
:
(t,\mathtt{y})=(\mathtt{Intro}_{A},\vec{0},\vec{0})\ ,\ (t^{\prime},\mathtt{y}^{\prime})=(\mathtt{Intro}_{B},\vec{0},\vec{0})
.

Operation
: Recall that
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
gets as input
a^{\mathfrak{R}},b^{\mathfrak{R}}
, which in this case are in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}+\Delta(n)}
, namely bit strings of length
2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}+\Delta(n)
. Denote by
que_{A}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}
(resp.
que_{B}
) the restriction of
a^{\mathfrak{R}}
(resp.
b^{\mathfrak{R}}
) to its first
2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}
bits, and
ans_{A}^{\mathfrak{R}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\Delta(n)}
(resp.
ans_{B}^{\mathfrak{R}}
) the restriction to its last
\Delta(n)
bits. Now,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
calls the original linear constraints processor
\mathcal{L}(n,que_{A},que_{B},ans^{\mathfrak{R}}_{A},ans^{\mathfrak{R}}_{B})
. If the decoding of
\mathcal{L}
’s output is not a sequence
(c^{1},...,c^{m})
of bit strings of length
4\Delta(n)+1
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
outputs
\mathfrak{error}
. Otherwise, each
c^{i}
is of the form
c^{i}=(c^{i}_{A,\mathfrak{R}},c^{i}_{A,\mathfrak{L}},c^{i}_{B,\mathfrak{R}},c^{i}_{B,\mathfrak{L}},c^{i}_{\mathsf{J}})
, where the first four (sub-)bit strings are of length
\Delta(n)
, and
c^{i}_{\mathsf{J}}
is of length
1
. Then,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
outputs (the encoding) of the sequence of bit strings
(\tilde{c}^{1},...,\tilde{c}^{m})
, where each
\tilde{c}^{i}=(\vec{0},c^{i}_{A,\mathfrak{R}},c^{i}_{A,\mathfrak{L}},\vec{0},c^{i}_{B,\mathfrak{R}},c^{i}_{B,\mathfrak{L}},c^{i}_{\mathsf{J}})
and
\vec{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}
.

Interpretation
: In this case
S^{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathtt{Intro}_{\cdot}}=\{\mathsf{Que}^{\cdot,i}\}_{i=1}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}\bigcup\{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot,\mathfrak{R},j}\}_{j=1}^{\Delta(n)}
and
S^{\mathfrak{L}}_{\mathtt{Intro}_{\cdot}}=\{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot,\mathfrak{L},j}\}_{j=1}^{\Delta(n)}
. Then,
que_{A}=\gamma(\mathsf{Que}^{A,i})_{i=1}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}},que_{B}=\gamma(\mathsf{Que}^{B,i})_{i=1}^{2^{|\mathcal{K}(n)|}}
are treated as a pair of questions in the original game, and
ans^{\mathfrak{R}}_{\cdot}=\gamma(\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot,\mathfrak{R},j})_{j=1}^{\Delta(n)}
as the respective readable parts of answers. Thus
L_{que_{A},que_{B}}(ans^{\mathfrak{R}}_{A},ans^{\mathfrak{R}}_{B})
induces linear constraints on the
4\Delta(n)
variables at the vertices
que_{A}
and
que_{B}
in the original game, which are checked instead on
\{\mathsf{Ans}^{\cdot,\cdot,j}\}_{j=1}^{\Delta(n)}
by

\widetilde{L}_{\mathtt{Intro}_{A},\mathtt{Intro}_{B}}(que_{A},ans^{\mathfrak{R}}_{A},que_{B},ans^{\mathfrak{R}}_{B})\;.
